❯ This notebook is a guide to making a VQC for a credic card fraud dataset with 
  about .17% positives in it. Sections 1-10 walk you through the basics of ML,  
  QC, how to create a baseline VQC and how noise influences it. I need to       
  bring at least an improvement to the baseline model, and I want to make an    
  improvement related to cheaper training. From a prior conversation with       
  Claude, I got these conclusions:                                              
  Thanks, that helps. I read all four. Reading them changes my earlier advice   
  in a few places.                                                              
                                                                                
  What the papers say                                                           
                                                                                
  Spall (SPSA, [11]): Each step needs only 2 loss evaluations, however many     
  parameters you have. The paper’s own numbers show the savings are real but    
  depend on the setting:                                                        
  At p = 20, SPSA reached FDSA’s final accuracy with roughly 1/20 to 1/10 of    
  the measurements.                                                             
  At p = 5 the advantage is much smaller.                                       
  Slowly decaying gains (a_k ∝ 1/k^0.75, c_k ∝ 1/k^0.25) beat the textbook 1/k  
  gain at finite sample sizes.                                                  
  Averaging q > 1 gradient estimates per step stabilizes training when noise is 
  large.                                                                        
  Spall warns that the asymptotic theory should be used with caution in         
  practice.                                                                     
  Kübler et al. (iCANS, [8–10]): SPSA was the most hyperparameter-sensitive     
  method they tested, and it did not beat iCANS or Adam in their shot-budget    
  comparisons. Their SPSA settings were β = 0.602, γ = 0.101, and A equal to    
  10% of the iterations, which match what I gave you. So the saving must be     
  demonstrated in circuit evaluations, not assumed.                             
  Sweke et al. ([8–10]):                                                        
  Mini-batch gradients are unbiased for MSE-type losses.                        
  The best strategy they found is to start with cheap, noisy updates, then      
  increase batch size or shots while decaying the learning rate.                
  They train cheaply and evaluate on the validation set once per epoch with     
  high-precision estimates, which is your “validate less often.”                
  Unbiasedness is proven for polynomial losses like MSE. Cross-entropy is       
  biased under finite-shot estimates.                                           
  Rosalin (Arrasmith et al., [8–10]): It samples Hamiltonian terms, and your    
  observable is a single Z, so it doesn’t apply directly. Its useful idea is    
  that weighted random sampling stays unbiased even at very low budgets. That   
  carries over to data sampling (below).                                        
  Layerwise training [12]: This wasn’t among the files, so I can’t ground it.   
                                                                                
  What I’d build                                                                
                                                                                
  Stratified, importance-weighted minibatches. At 0.17% positives, a uniform    
  batch of 32 contains a positive only about 5% of the time. Draw batches with  
  a fixed positive fraction, and weight each sample by (its weight in the       
  baseline loss) / (N × its sampling probability). This keeps the estimator     
  unbiased, following Sweke’s weighted-sampling remark. The papers don’t treat  
  imbalance, so this part is my addition.                                       
  SPSA with the same minibatch for both evaluations, q = 2–4 averaged           
  estimates, Bernoulli ±1 perturbations, and the slow-decay gains. Tune a and c 
  on a short pilot run, since the papers flag the sensitivity.                  
  A Sweke-style hybrid as the fallback or comparison. Run SPSA or small batches 
  first, then switch to the baseline gradient with larger batches and           
  learning-rate decay for the final stretch.                                    
  Cheap validation. Evaluate every k steps on a fixed stratified subset, and    
  stop on validation AUPRC. If you subsample negatives, AUPRC is inflated       
  because precision depends on prevalence. Pass sample_weight = 1/sampling_rate 
  for the negatives to average_precision_score, or use the subset only as a     
  relative stopping signal. Report final AUPRC on the full test set.            
                                                                                
  How to show it stays close                                                    
                                                                                
  Cost: Plot AUPRC against cumulative circuit evaluations, with the baseline’s  
  cost counted the same way.                                                    
  Ranking agreement: Report Spearman rho between the two models’ test scores.   
  The 99.8% negatives dominate it, so also report top-k overlap (for example,   
  the top 0.5% of scores) and AUPRC.                                            
  Seeds: The test set has only a few hundred positives, so AUPRC is noisy. Use  
  3–5 seeds and bootstrap confidence intervals.                                 
  Noise sweep: Rerun your existing noise sweep on the cheap model.              
  . Can you implement the four sugestions in the section 11 template part?

● I changed the evaluate_under_noise cell (Part 11.2) in the notebook. That's
  the only change to the notebook so far.

  - What changed: y_valid and y_test_part now default to None, and the function
    picks up the current y_val, y_test each time you call it. It also checks
    that inputs and labels have the same length first. If they don't, you get a
    message telling you to restart and run all, instead of a sklearn error from
    deep inside precision_recall_curve.
  - What it doesn't change: it doesn't fix your current kernel by itself. Z_val,
    Z_test and weights are still from the 30% run. Restart the kernel and run
    everything from Part 0 with SAMPLE_FRACTION = 1.0.
  - Reload the notebook in your editor first. I edited the file on disk. If your
    open copy saves over it, my change is lost.

  The Section 11.4 cells (the four cheap-training changes) aren't in the
  notebook yet. Their 5-seed test run is still going in the background, and I'll
  insert them once it passes.

      210 +class StratifiedSampler:                                      
      211 +    """Minibatch-uri cu o proportie fixa de fraude, extrase di
          +n TOT train-ul (dezechilibrat).                               
      212 +                                                              
      213 +    Ponderea share_c / n_c pe fiecare exemplu din clasa c face
          + loss-ul ponderat un estimator                                
      214 +    NEDEPLASAT al loss-ului echilibrat 0.5 * media(legit) + 0.
          +5 * media(fraude),                                            
      215 +    adica exact loss-ul pe care il estimeaza baseline-ul cu un
          +dersampling."""                                               
      216 +    def __init__(self, y, batch_size, pos_fraction, seed):    
      217 +        self.pools = [np.flatnonzero(y == 0), np.flatnonzero(y
          + == 1)]                                                       
      218 +        n_pos = int(np.clip(round(pos_fraction * batch_size), 
          +1, batch_size - 1))                                           
      219 +        self.counts = [batch_size - n_pos, n_pos]             
      220 +        self.weights = torch.tensor(np.concatenate([np.full(n,
          + 0.5 / n) for n in self.counts]))                             
      221 +        self.rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)                
      222 +                                                              
      223 +    def draw(self):                                           
      224 +        idx = np.concatenate([self.rng.choice(pool, size=n, re
          +place=False)                                                  
      225 +                              for pool, n in zip(self.pools, s
          +elf.counts)])                                                 
      226 +        return idx, self.weights                              
      227 +                                                              
      228 +                                                              
      229 +def stratified_val_subset(y_val, neg_rate, seed):             
      230 +    """Toate fraudele + o fractiune fixa din legitime. Pondere
          +a 1/neg_rate pe legitime                                      
      231 +    corecteaza AUPRC-ul (precizia depinde de prevalenta). Folo
          +sit DOAR pentru early stopping."""                            
      232 +    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)                         
      233 +    pos, neg = np.flatnonzero(y_val == 1), np.flatnonzero(y_va
          +l == 0)                                                       
      234 +    kept = rng.choice(neg, size=max(1, round(neg_rate * len(ne
          +g))), replace=False)                                          
      235 +    subset = np.sort(np.concatenate((pos, kept)))             
      236 +    return subset, np.where(y_val[subset] == 1, 1.0, len(neg) 
          +/ len(kept))                                                  
      237 +                                                              
      238 +                                                              
      239 +def spsa_gradient(loss_of, weights, c, q, generator):         
      240 +    """Media a q estimari SPSA; fiecare = 2 evaluari ale loss-
          +ului pe ACELASI minibatch."""                                 
      241 +    g = torch.zeros_like(weights)                             
      242 +    with torch.no_grad():                                     
      243 +        for _ in range(q):                                    
      244 +            delta = 2 * torch.randint(0, 2, weights.shape, gen
          +erator=generator).to(weights.dtype) - 1                       
      245 +            g += (loss_of(weights + c * delta) - loss_of(weigh
          +ts - c * delta)) / (2 * c) * delta                            
      246 +    return g / q                                              
      247 +                                                              
      248 +                                                              
      249 +def train_spsa(circuit, cfg, Z_train, y_train, Z_val, y_val, s
          +eed, quick=False):                                            
      250 +    """SPSA (Spall): castiguri a_k = a/(k+1+A)^0.602, c_k = c/
          +(k+1)^0.101, A = 10% din pasi.                                
      251 +    Intoarce si executiile de circuit numarate efectiv (traini
          +ng + validare)."""                                            
      252 +    set_seed(seed)                                            
      253 +    qnode = qml.QNode(circuit, qml.device("default.qubit", wir
          +es=cfg["n_qubits"]),                                          
      254 +                      interface="torch", diff_method="backprop
          +")                                                            
      255 +    weights = init_weights(seed, cfg).detach().clone()        
      256 +    Xt, yt = torch.tensor(Z_train), torch.tensor(y_train, dtyp
          +e=torch.float64)                                              
      257 +    batch_size = cfg["batch_size"]                            
      258 +    sampler = StratifiedSampler(y_train, batch_size, cfg["pos_
          +fraction"], seed)                                             
      259 +    generator = torch.Generator().manual_seed(seed)           
      260 +    subset, sub_weights = stratified_val_subset(y_val, cfg["va
          +l_neg_rate"], seed)                                           
      261 +    Z_sub, y_sub = Z_val[subset], y_val[subset]               
      262 +    steps = 100 if quick else cfg["spsa_steps"]               
      263 +    exact_steps = min(cfg["exact_steps"], 10) if quick else cf
          +g["exact_steps"]                                              
      264 +    runs = dict(train=0, val=0)                               
      265 +    best = dict(ap=-1.0, weights=weights.clone(), step=0, sinc
          +e=0)                                                          
      266 +                                                              
      267 +    def minibatch_loss(s):                                    
      268 +        idx, w = s.draw()                                     
      269 +        return lambda v: torch.nn.functional.binary_cross_entr
          +opy(                                                          
      270 +            to_score(qnode(Xt[idx], v)), yt[idx], weight=w, re
          +duction="sum")                                                
      271 +                                                              
      272 +    def validate(step):                                       
      273 +        runs["val"] += len(Z_sub)                             
      274 +        ap = average_precision_score(y_sub, predict(qnode, Z_s
          +ub, weights), sample_weight=sub_weights)                      
      275 +        if ap > best["ap"]:                                   
      276 +            best.update(ap=ap, weights=weights.detach().clone(
          +), step=step, since=0)                                        
      277 +        else:                                                 
      278 +            best["since"] += 1                                
      279 +        return best["since"] >= cfg["patience"]  # True = opri
          +re                                                            
      280 +                                                              
      281 +    # Calibrare (regula lui Spall): primul pas muta fiecare pa
          +rametru cu ~spsa_target_step radiani.                         
      282 +    A, c = 0.1 * steps, cfg["spsa_c"]                         
      283 +    g0 = spsa_gradient(minibatch_loss(sampler), weights, c, 10
          +, generator)                                                  
      284 +    runs["train"] += 2 * 10 * batch_size                      
      285 +    a = cfg["spsa_target_step"] * (A + 1) ** 0.602 / max(g0.ab
          +s().mean().item(), 1e-12)                                     
      286 +    step = 0                                                  
      287 +    for step in range(1, steps + 1):                          
      288 +        g = spsa_gradient(minibatch_loss(sampler), weights, c 
          +/ step ** 0.101, cfg["spsa_q"], generator)                    
      289 +        weights = weights - a / (step + A) ** 0.602 * g       
      290 +        runs["train"] += 2 * cfg["spsa_q"] * batch_size       
      291 +        if step % cfg["val_every"] == 0 and validate(step):   
      292 +            break                                             
      293 +                                                              
      294 +    # Optional (hibrid, Sweke et al.): gradient exact, batch m
          +ai mare, learning rate descrescator.                          
      295 +    if exact_steps > 0:                                       
      296 +        weights = torch.nn.Parameter(best["weights"].clone()) 
      297 +        best["since"] = 0                                     
      298 +        optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([weights], lr=cfg["exact_
          +lr"])                                                         
      299 +        scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.LambdaLR(optimize
          +r, lambda s: 1 - s / exact_steps)                             
      300 +        exact_sampler = StratifiedSampler(y_train, cfg["exact_
          +batch_size"], cfg["pos_fraction"], seed + 1)                  
      301 +        for s in range(1, exact_steps + 1):                   
      302 +            optimizer.zero_grad()                             
      303 +            minibatch_loss(exact_sampler)(weights).backward() 
      304 +            optimizer.step()                                  
      305 +            scheduler.step()                                  
      306 +            runs["train"] += cfg["exact_batch_size"] * (1 + 2 
          +* weights.numel())  # formula 1+2P                            
      307 +            if s % 10 == 0 and validate(step + s):            
      308 +                break                                         
      309 +    return best["weights"], best["step"], runs                
      310 +

● SPSA is now in main.py, as a third model, improved_spsa. python3 main.py 
  --quick runs end to end. The full 5-seed run hasn't been done yet, so there
  are no real accuracy numbers for it.

  How it works
  - It uses the same circuit as your improved model (fewer gates). Only the
    training changes, so you can compare improved and improved_spsa directly.
  - Setting optimizer="spsa" in a config sends it to the new train_spsa
    function. All its settings are in the MODELS block.
  - Training batches: it trains on minibatches from the full training set, each
    half frauds. Per-example weights make the loss match, on average, the
    balanced loss the baseline trains on.
  - SPSA: two loss evaluations per estimate on the same batch, two estimates
    averaged per step, and slowly shrinking step sizes. The initial step size
    and perturbation (spsa_target_step=0.03, spsa_c=0.05) are the values the
    pilot picked in my notebook test.
  - Validation: every 50 steps it scores a fixed subset: all validation frauds
    plus 5% of the legitimate ones, weighted so AUPRC isn't inflated. It stops
    after 6 checks without improvement and keeps the best weights. The final
    evaluation still uses your full validation and test sets.
  - Hybrid option: exact_steps > 0 adds a short stretch of exact-gradient
    training at the end. It is off by default.
  - Cost: circuit_cost now takes the executions SPSA actually counted. The two
    Adam models still use the 1+2P formula, so the cost table compares them
    fairly.

  Quick run: about 13.6k executions for improved_spsa against about 92.7k for
  improved. Its AUPRCs mean nothing: --quick uses 3 epochs, 100 SPSA steps, and
  a validation sample with only about 3 frauds.

  What to expect on the full run
  - In my notebook test (baseline circuit, 5 seeds), SPSA used about 3% of the
    baseline's executions.
  - But mean test AUPRC fell from 0.58 to 0.49, with a large spread: two of five
    seeds were clearly worse, one was better.
  - Early stopping on the noisy validation subset seemed to stop some runs too
    soon. That's why patience is 6 here instead of 4, but I haven't tested that
    change.
  - If the gap stays, try patience=99 (always run all 600 steps) or
    val_neg_rate=0.2 (a less noisy subset).

  The quick run also created task2_runs.csv, task2_summary.csv and
  task2_cost.csv in the repo folder. Your full run will overwrite them.

※ recap: Goal: show cheaper training for your VQC by adding SPSA to main.py; the full 5-seed run is going in the background. Next, once it finishes, I'll summarise accuracy and cost against the baseline and improved models. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ I got some more sugestions from the other conversation:                       
  Noise-aware SPSA. Train inside the depolarizing model, which the rules        
  explicitly allow, and compare against train-noiseless/deploy-noisy. SPSA      
  needs only forward passes, so it avoids backpropagating through density       
  matrices, which is the slow part of noise-aware training. A cheap variant is  
  to warm-start from the noiseless SPSA result, then fine-tune for a few        
  hundred steps at p = 0.01, or sample p at random each step (noise injection,  
  as in QuantumNAT [16]). The cost ledger stays the same as for noiseless       
  training, because noise is free on a simulator but also on the hardware you   
  are budgeting for.                                                            
  Light-cone pruning and gate merging. The brief says 12 of the baseline’s 32   
  parameters never reach the readout. Remove those gates, plus any CNOTs that   
  cannot affect Z on the readout wire. Then try PennyLane’s merge_rotations and 
  cancel_inverses transforms, and check their current names in the docs. This   
  cuts noise locations and the 1+2P term in the baseline ledger, and it         
  combines with every other idea here.                                          
  . Can you implement these two sugestions?                                     

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both transforms exist in PennyLane 0.45 and work with batched inputs. The full
  run is still on seed 0, and editing main.py won't affect it because Python
  has already loaded the file. Now the edits:

❯ Can you check if the torch functions use my GPU or not? This might be causing 
  the slow runs.                                                                

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● No, nothing runs on your GPU. All the torch work is on the CPU, for three
  reasons:

  1. PyTorch can't see the GPU. Your torch is 2.14.1+cu130 (built for CUDA
     13.0), but the installed NVIDIA driver only supports CUDA 12.4. That's the
     "driver too old" warning in every run's log. torch.cuda.is_available() is
     False.
  2. The driver itself isn't responding right now. nvidia-smi fails with
     "couldn't communicate with the NVIDIA driver". Most likely the NVIDIA
     kernel module isn't loaded for your current kernel (6.19), so the GPU is
     unusable even without the torch mismatch.
  3. The code never asks for a GPU. main.py creates plain default.qubit /
     default.mixed devices and CPU tensors, with no .to("cuda") anywhere.

  But I doubt the GPU is the cause of the slowness, and fixing it probably 
  wouldn't help much. At 4 qubits a density matrix is only 16×16. The time goes
  to PennyLane applying one small operation after another (126 gates plus their
  noise channels for the baseline), each with Python overhead. A GPU is good at
  big matrix operations, and launching that many tiny ones on it is often slower
  than the CPU. I haven't measured this here, though.

  The likely cause of the slow runs is the two jobs fighting over the CPU. Each
  Python process starts 20 torch threads, and with the full run and my quick
  check running together the load average is about 36 on 20 cores. Thread
  oversubscription like that can slow both jobs badly. What would help:
  - run one job at a time (the quick check should finish soon);
  - or cap threads per process, e.g. OMP_NUM_THREADS=4 python3 main.py --models 
    ... --out ..., and run models or seeds in parallel as separate processes.
    Small 4-qubit simulations usually don't gain much beyond a few threads.

  To get the GPU working anyway: either install a torch build that matches your
  driver (pip install torch --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu124),
  or update the NVIDIA driver, and make sure the kernel module loads. Then
  you'd also need GPU-capable PennyLane devices or code changes, which I'd only
  try after measuring that it actually helps at 4 qubits.

● Both suggestions are now in main.py, and the quick check ran end to end for
  all four new models. That covers the noise-aware path: the warm start was
  re-scored under noise, then the noisy SPSA steps ran and were validated at p =
  0.01. The AUPRC numbers from that run mean nothing (3 epochs or 100 SPSA
  steps, and about 3 frauds in the validation sample).

  Light-cone pruning and gate merging
  - In a config, prune=True removes every gate that can't influence ⟨Z⟩ on the
    readout qubit. That includes the final RZ on the readout and CNOTs
    controlled from it at the end.
  - The pruned circuits give the same outputs as the originals (differences
    around 1e-16). Since the outputs are identical, training is identical too:
    pruned scored exactly the same as improved in the quick runs, at less than
    half the training cost (40.7k vs 86.7k executions).

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────┬───────┬─────────────────┐
  │           circuit           │ parameters │ gates │ noise locations │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┤
  │ baseline                    │ 32         │ 93    │ 126             │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┤
  │ baseline + pruning          │ 19         │ 77    │ 107             │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┤
  │ improved                    │ 24         │ 56    │ 74              │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┤
  │ pruned (improved + pruning) │ 11         │ 40    │ 55              │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────┴───────┴─────────────────┘

  - merge=True applies qml.transforms.merge_rotations and
    qml.transforms.cancel_inverses (those are the current names in PennyLane
    0.45). On these circuits they change nothing: after pruning there are no
    adjacent rotations or inverse pairs left. They stay in as an option for
    other architectures.
  - The cost table now counts P as the parameters actually left in the circuit.
    Unpruned models are unchanged; the baseline is still 32.

  Noise-aware SPSA
  - A noise_train config option runs a second SPSA stage inside the official
    depolarizing model, starting from the noiseless SPSA result.
  - Checkpoints are chosen on the validation subset at p = 0.01.
  - The warm-start weights are scored under noise first. If fine-tuning doesn't
    beat them, they are what you get.
  - Executions are counted the same way as noiseless training.
  - Two models use it:
    - pruned_spsa_p01 trains at a fixed p = 0.01.
    - pruned_spsa_nat picks p at random from {0, 0.01, 0.05} at each step, which
      is the noise-injection idea.

  New models and options
  - New models: pruned, pruned_spsa, pruned_spsa_p01 and pruned_spsa_nat.
  - New command-line options: --models and --out. They let you run only the new
    models without overwriting the main CSVs, for example: python3 main.py 
    --models pruned,pruned_spsa,pruned_spsa_p01,pruned_spsa_nat --out task2_new.
  - I deleted the quick check's CSVs (scratch_quick_*).

  Timing
  - The quick check took 20 minutes because it shared the CPU with the full run.

    covers baseline, improved and improved_spsa. The new models need a second
    run.

    as OMP_NUM_THREADS=4, so the two don't slow each other down again. Should I
    start it?

● Yes, the script uses the common split from Part 11.1 (I'm reading "common
  script" as the common split in split_indices.npz).

  How the script uses it: load_split() in main.py loads split_indices.npz from
  the folder next to the script and takes train, validation and test with
  X.iloc[...]. That is equivalent to the X_all.loc[split[...]] snippet in the
  notebook, because the dataframe's index is the plain row numbers 0…N−1.

  I checked your file against the notebook's split:
  - I recomputed the Part 4 split (SEED = 42, SAMPLE_FRACTION = 1.0) and
    compared it with split_indices.npz.
  - Train (170,883 rows), validation (56,962) and test (56,962) are identical:
    same rows, in the same order.
  - The file dates from 14:02, when you ran the Part 11.1 cell, so it was saved
    from the full-data run, not the 30% one.

  Things to keep in mind:
  - If split_indices.npz is missing, the script recreates it with the same two
    train_test_split calls. As written that gives the same split, but it's safer
    to always ship the notebook's file with the script.
  - The script only checks that the three parts add up to the full dataset. It
    doesn't check the labels like the notebook's assert does. That's fine as
    long as the file stays this one.
  - --quick keeps the same training rows but cuts validation and test down to
    2,000 rows each, so quick results aren't on the common split. The full run
    uses the split as is.

  suggested direction. Your improved_spsa model already does a version of it:

  the undersampled baseline optimizes, so what remains is making the weighting  
  tunable and testing it.

  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ With loss_pos_share=0.5 this reproduces your current behavior. Lower values   
  such as 0.2 or 0.1 weight the loss closer to the true prior while keeping     
  positives frequent in each batch. Add loss_pos_share to the improved_spsa     
  config and pass it to both samplers.                                          
                                                                                
  2. Why it’s worth an ablation                                                 
                                                                                
  A fully balanced loss distorts probabilities (the Dal Pozzolo paper in your   
  references). Your threshold is chosen on validation scores and AUPRC is       
  rank-based, so that matters less here. A milder weighting can still improve   
  the ranking among the highest-scored negatives, which is where precision at   
  high recall is decided.                                                       
  It also changes SPSA’s noise. Heavier weights on rare positives mean larger   
  variance per step.                                                            
                                                                                
  3. Suggested grid                                                             
                                                                                
  Use loss_pos_share in {0.5, 0.25, 0.1} and keep pos_fraction at 0.5. Run the  
  same 5 seeds and report AUPRC at p = 0.01 and 0.05, Spearman ρ, and F1. Tune  
  on validation, never on test. The ledger doesn’t change, since the number of  
  executions per step is the same.                                              
  This is the rest of the message.

● I've added the class-weight setting to main.py. Nothing new is running; the
  earlier runs are still going.

  What changed
  - New setting loss_pos_share (default 0.5). It sets how much the frauds weigh
    in the loss, separately from pos_fraction, which sets how many frauds are in
    each batch.
  - StratifiedSampler uses it as follows:
    - the objective is (1 − share) · mean(legit loss) + share · mean(fraud 
      loss);
    - each row gets weight share_c / n_c;
    - both the SPSA sampler and the hybrid's exact-gradient sampler use it.
  - With 0.5 the behaviour is exactly what you had before.
  - Two new models for the grid, both with pos_fraction=0.5:
    - improved_spsa_share25
    - improved_spsa_share10
    - improved_spsa itself is the 0.5 point.
  - The cost per step doesn't change, so the ledger stays the same.

  Check (sampler only, no circuits)
  - At every share the average batch loss matches its target: 1.648 vs 1.649,
    1.2238 vs 1.2239, and 0.9690 vs 0.9690.
  - The spread per batch grows from 0.050 to 0.057 to 0.066 as the share drops.
    That's the extra SPSA noise the suggestion predicts.

  Worth knowing before the ablation
  - pruned_spsa and improved_spsa will end up with identical trained weights.
    The pruned gates have no effect on the output, so SPSA's loss values and
    updates are the same step for step.
  - They differ only under noise: the pruned circuit has 55 noise locations
    instead of 74.
  - So the class-weight grid would arguably be more useful on pruned_spsa. Your
    strongest circuit would then also get the best weighting, and the noise
    results would be cleaner.
  - Moving it is a one-line change per model; tell me if you want that.

  Current list (9 models): baseline, improved, improved_spsa, pruned,
  pruned_spsa, pruned_spsa_p01, pruned_spsa_nat, improved_spsa_share25 and
  improved_spsa_share10. When you're done adding models, I'll run the missing
  ones with the thread cap. Then I'll merge everything into one table with AUPRC
  at p = 0.01 and p = 0.05, Spearman ρ and F1 as mean ± std over 5 seeds, plus
  the cost ledger.

● I checked main.py against the evaluation protocol and deliverables in the
  brief. Most of it already matches. There are three gaps worth fixing before
  the large ablation.

  Already matching the protocol

  Requirement in the brief: Common split; balancing, encoding, epochs and
  threshold chosen on train/validation only
  Status in main.py: ✓ Loads split_indices.npz (checked identical to the
  notebook's). The encoder is fitted on training rows only. The test set is
  used only in the final scoring.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement in the brief: Noise: decompose to {RX, RY, RZ, Rot, H, CNOT, CZ},
  depolarizing after every gate on every qubit, density matrices, p ∈ {0,
  0.001, 0.01, 0.05}
  Status in main.py: ✓ noisy_qnode is the notebook's, and NOISE lists the
  official levels. Pruned circuits are decomposed and evaluated whole, as
  required.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement in the brief: Threshold that maximises F1 on validation at the
  same
  p
  Status in main.py: ✓ Done in evaluate, once per p
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement in the brief: Metrics: test AUPRC (headline at p = 0.01 and 0.05),

  AUROC, Spearman ρ noisy vs noiseless, precision, recall, F1, MCC; mean ± std
  over ≥ 5 seeds
  Status in main.py: ✓ All in *_runs.csv and *_summary.csv
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement in the brief: Baseline = the Task 1 VQC over 5 seeds
  Status in main.py: ✓ Same architecture and training. My notebook run gave
  0.580
  ± 0.053 noiseless, which matches the brief's 0.58 ± 0.05.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement in the brief: Classical reference on the same inputs
  Status in main.py: ✓ Logistic regression on the same 4 angles, for every model
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement in the brief: Cost ledger, training and validation, against the
  baseline
  Status in main.py: ✓ The baseline formula reproduces the brief's 1,150,500 and

  1,708,860 executions (30 × 590 × 65 and 30 × 56,962). SPSA models report the
  executions they actually counted.

  Gaps worth fixing

  1. No validation metrics are saved, so the ablation can't pick a winner 
     without peeking at test. The brief says choices are "fitted or chosen on
     training and validation data only", and judging includes "results not
     cherry-picked". With 9+ models and a class-weight grid, the final model and
     the loss_pos_share value must be picked on validation. Right now the CSVs
     only contain test metrics. Fix: also save validation AUPRC per p in
     evaluate. The noisy validation scores are already computed there, so it
     costs nothing.
  2. The SPSA pilot isn't counted in the cost ledger. spsa_target_step and
     spsa_c come from the notebook pilot, which cost about 172k executions on
     the baseline circuit, and the script never runs it. Either add it to the
     ledger as a one-off cost, or put the pilot in the script so it's
     reproducible end to end and counted.
  3. There is no single final table. The deliverable is code "runnable end to
     end with fixed seeds, from the dataset and the common split to the final
     tables". With the per-model --out files, the results are spread over
     several CSVs. A small merge step (or one plain python main.py over all
     models) should produce the final tables: every model and the classical
     reference at every noise level, plus the cost ledger.

  Smaller points (for the presentation, not violations)
  - The logistic regression is always trained on the 590 undersampled rows. For
    the SPSA and class-weight models, which train on the full training set, a
    class-weighted logistic regression on the full set would be the fairer
    reference.
  - The judges explicitly reward reaching "the same performance for less" and
    "stability across seeds". So the SPSA comparison should show the cost
    alongside the AUPRC spread, as the notebook check did with bootstrap
    intervals and top-k overlap. The script doesn't compute those yet.
  - Only the brief requires reporting every noise level, but the script's
    console summary prints only AUPRC, ρ and F1. The full metrics are in the
    CSV.

  The background runs aren't affected by any of this. They still produce valid
  test results; they just lack the validation columns from point 1. If you want,
  I'll fix points 1–3 now, before you add more models. Point 1 would mean
  rerunning the models that are already running to get their validation numbers.

● I fixed points 1–3 in main.py and didn't rerun anything. The four runs still
  going use the version of main.py that was loaded at start, so they're
  unaffected, and their CSVs won't have the new columns.

  1. Validation metrics are saved
  - Every row in *_runs.csv now has val_AUPRC: the validation AUPRC at the same
    noise level, from the scores evaluate already computes for the threshold.
    The logistic regression rows have it too.
  - The report uses it to recommend the best VQC at p = 0.01 and p = 0.05 by
    validation, never by test. Models without validation data are left out of
    that choice, and the printout says so.

  2. The SPSA pilot runs inside the script
  - With pilot=True (on by default in the SPSA config), each SPSA model first
    runs a short pilot.
  - The pilot is 150 steps for each pair in pilot_grid: step size {0.01, 0.03,
    0.1} × c {0.05, 0.15}.
  - It runs on the first seed and uses only training and validation data. The
    best pair is then used for all 5 seeds.
  - Its executions appear in the ledger as a one-off cost
    (pilot_executions_one_off), and the grid results are saved in
    {out}_pilot.csv.
  - pilot=False uses the fixed values from the config instead.

  3. Final tables
  - New function report(). It merges any set of runs into:
    - *_final_results.csv: every model and its classical reference at every p,
      all protocol metrics plus val_AUPRC, mean ± std and the number of seeds;
    - *_final_cost.csv: parameters, gates, noise locations, mean training and
      validation executions, shots, the one-off pilot cost, and the ratio to the
      baseline.
  - A plain python main.py produces the final tables at the end, so the script
    is runnable end to end as the brief requires.
  - To merge separate runs: python main.py --report task2,task2_pruned,....

  What I checked
  - report on your existing quick-run CSVs (old format), plus a temporary copy
    with validation columns to test the model choice. I deleted the copy
    afterwards.
  - spsa_pilot by itself on small synthetic data with a tiny grid, which ran in
    seconds.
  - I haven't run a real model with the pilot. The first full run will be the
    first real test.

  From the running jobs, so far:
  - On seed 0, pruned_spsa_p01 and pruned_spsa_nat both ended at step 350. That
    is where the warm start was scored under noise, and their AUPRC at p = 0.05
    (0.3793) equals improved_spsa's.
  - So the noise-aware fine-tuning never beat its starting point on validation,
    and both kept the noiseless SPSA weights.
  - It's one seed, but if it repeats, either the fine-tune step
    (target_step=0.01) is too small or the noisy validation subset is too noisy
    to detect gains. That's worth considering while you look for more ideas.

  │ pruned          │ 0.552  │ 0.541  │ 0.521  │ 0.538 ± 0.016 (3) │

  ├─────────────────┼────────┼────────┼────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ pruned_spsa     │ 0.379  │ 0.500  │ 0.503  │ 0.461 ± 0.071 (3) │
  ├─────────────────┼────────┼────────┼────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ pruned_spsa_p01 │ 0.379  │ 0.509  │ –      │ 0.444 (2)         │
  ├─────────────────┼────────┼────────┼────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ pruned_spsa_nat │ 0.379  │ 0.493  │ –      │ 0.436 (2)         │
  └─────────────────┴────────┴────────┴────────┴───────────────────┘

  What this suggests so far:
  - The smaller circuit is the clear gain. improved/pruned beat the baseline at
    p = 0.05 on every seed where both have finished (0.552 vs 0.421, 0.541 vs
    0.514), with a small spread.
  - Light-cone pruning doesn't change the noisy results at all, and that's 
    expected. pruned and improved give exactly 0.5515, as do pruned_spsa and
    improved_spsa (0.3793). The removed gates either sit outside the readout's
    light cone, so their noise can't reach qubit 0, or they come at the very end
    on the readout. Noise there only scales ⟨Z⟩ by a constant, which leaves the
    ranking (AUPRC, ρ) unchanged, and the validation threshold absorbs it. So
    "126 → 107 noise locations" overstates the benefit. Pruning's real gain is
    cost: P drops from 24 to 11 in 1+2P, about half the training executions.
    That's worth saying in the presentation, and it's a good research-style
    point.
  - SPSA is lower and less stable (0.46 ± 0.07) for roughly 3% of the
    executions. On seeds 1–2 it's within about 0.04 of pruned; seed 0 is the
    weak one.
  - Noise-aware fine-tuning hasn't shown a gain yet. On seed 0 both variants
    kept the warm start (identical 0.379). On seed 1 they land within ±0.01 of
    plain SPSA, which is noise-level.

  Still pending:
  - The first run is slow (only seed 1 after 57 minutes). The baseline circuit's
    noisy evaluation is the heaviest part, and six processes share the CPU.
  - The quick run of all 9 current models is in the background. It checks that
    the pilot, the class-weight models, the report and the figures work end to
    end. Its metrics won't mean anything, so I'll report whether it works and
    what the figures look like. I'll update this table when the full runs
    finish.

      474 +def tracker_per_example(cfg, batch_size=32):                  
      475 +    """Executiile masurate cu qml.Tracker pentru UN gradient p
          +arameter-shift, pe exemplu,                                   
      476 +    cu SHOTS shots. Depinde doar de structura circuitului: Pen
          +nyLane nu shifteaza parametrii                                
      477 +    care nu pot influenta masuratoarea, deci de obicei e sub f
          +ormula 1+2P."""                                               
      478 +    key = repr(sorted(cfg.items()))                           
      479 +    if key not in _TRACKER_CACHE:                             
      480 +        rng = np.random.default_rng(0)                        
      481 +        x = torch.tensor(rng.random((batch_size, cfg["n_qubits
          +"])) * np.pi)                                                 
      482 +        y = torch.tensor((np.arange(batch_size) % 2).astype(fl
          +oat))                                                         
      483 +        dev = qml.device("default.qubit", wires=cfg["n_qubits"
          +], seed=0)                                                    
      484 +        qnode = qml.set_shots(qml.QNode(make_circuit(cfg), dev
          +, interface="torch",                                          
      485 +                                        diff_method="parameter
          +-shift"), shots=SHOTS)                                        
      486 +        with qml.Tracker(dev) as tracker:                     
      487 +            torch.nn.functional.binary_cross_entropy(to_score(
          +qnode(x, init_weights(0, cfg))), y).backward()                
      488 +        _TRACKER_CACHE[key] = tracker.totals["executions"] / b
          +atch_size                                                     
      489 +    return _TRACKER_CACHE[key]

  Per-seed table: report now writes *_final_per_seed.csv (model × seed rows with
  AUPRC, Spearman ρ and F1 at p = 0, 0.001, 0.01 and 0.05) and prints the AUPRC
  version.

  Cost via qml.Tracker: the ledger now shows both counts side by side: the 1+2P
  formula, and the executions qml.Tracker measures for one parameter-shift
  gradient at 1,024 shots. For runs that started before this change, report
  works the Tracker count out from the circuit structure, so nothing needs
  rerunning. Per example:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────┐
  │               circuit                │ formula 1+2P │ Tracker │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ baseline                             │ 65           │ 41      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ improved                             │ 49           │ 25      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ pruned                               │ 23           │ 23      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┤
  │ SPSA step, validation (forward only) │ 1            │ 1       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────┘

  - So with Tracker counting, the baseline costs 30 × 590 × 41 = 725,700
    training executions, not 1,150,500.
  - improved and pruned are almost the same (25 vs 23 per example), because
    PennyLane already skips the parameters that can't reach the readout.
    Pruning's cost saving only shows up if you use the formula.
  - SPSA's savings don't depend on which count you use. Its training is forward
    passes only, so both counts agree.

❯ Can you sumarize the pruning implementation?                                  

● Here is how light-cone pruning works in main.py (light_cone, make_circuit,
  trainable_parameters).

  Idea
  The circuit measures only ⟨Z⟩ on the readout qubit (readout_wire, qubit 0). A
  gate that can never influence that measurement can be removed without changing
  any output, for any input and any weights.

  How it works
  1. Record the gates without running them. make_circuit builds the full circuit
     (feature map + ansatz) inside qml.queuing.AnnotatedQueue(). That gives the
     ordered list of gates, nothing is applied yet.
  2. Walk backwards from the measurement (light_cone). Start with the set of
     "live" wires = {readout}, then go through the gates from last to first:
     - a gate that touches no live wire is dropped;
     - a gate that is kept makes all of its wires live (a CNOT whose target is
       live makes its control live too);
     - one extra rule applies only while the observable is still exactly Z on
       the readout, that is, before any gate has been kept. Gates that commute
       with Z are dropped: diagonal gates (RZ, CZ, …) and CNOTs controlled from
       the readout. In practice this removes the final RZ on qubit 0.
  3. Apply only the kept gates with qml.apply, then measure as before.
  4. Optional merge=True wraps the circuit in qml.transforms.merge_rotations and
     qml.transforms.cancel_inverses.
  5. Parameter count for the cost formula (trainable_parameters): builds the
     circuit's operation list with weights marked as requiring gradients and
     counts the gates that still carry a weight. That count is the P in 1+2P.

  You switch it on per model with prune=True (and merge=True); the models
  pruned, pruned_spsa, pruned_spsa_p01 and pruned_spsa_nat use it.

  What it removes

  ┌───────────────────┬────────────┬─────────┬─────────────────┐
  │      circuit      │ parameters │  gates  │ noise locations │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ baseline → pruned │ 32 → 19    │ 93 → 77 │ 126 → 107       │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ improved → pruned │ 24 → 11    │ 56 → 40 │ 74 → 55         │
  └───────────────────┴────────────┴─────────┴─────────────────┘

  The 19 parameters left in the pruned baseline match the notebook's Part 8
  statement that only 19 matter.

  How it was checked
  - Outputs of the original and pruned circuits differ by about 1e-16 on 200
    random inputs, for both the baseline and improved.
  - The full runs confirm it: pruned and improved give exactly the same test
    AUPRC (0.5515 on seed 0), and so do pruned_spsa and improved_spsa.

  What it actually buys (be careful in the presentation)
  1. No accuracy gain under noise. Every removed gate either sits outside the
     readout's light cone, so its depolarizing noise can't reach qubit 0, or
     comes at the very end on the readout. Noise there only shrinks ⟨Z⟩ by a
     constant factor, which leaves the ranking (AUPRC, Spearman ρ) unchanged,
     and the threshold chosen on validation absorbs it. So the drop in noise
     locations (74 → 55) is real on paper but has no effect on the results. This
     is an honest negative result worth presenting.
  2. A cost gain only with the formula count. With 1+2P, training executions per
     example drop from 49 to 23 (about 2× cheaper). But qml.Tracker shows
     PennyLane already skips parameters that can't reach the measurement: 25
     executions per example for improved, 23 for pruned. With Tracker counting,
     pruning saves only about 8%.
  3. Merging does nothing here. After pruning there are no adjacent rotations or
     inverse pairs left. The option stays for other architectures.

  Where it would help: on hardware, smaller compiled circuits mean less run time
  and fewer gates to calibrate. It would also matter for circuits whose readout
  uses only part of the qubits, or with a noise model where errors spread
  across qubits (crosstalk), neither of which the challenge's noise model
  includes.

✻ Churned for 14s · done Saturday 16:43

❯ Based on the results so far, I should focus on the improved and pruned        
  models, right?                                                                

● Mostly yes, with three adjustments.

  1. improved and pruned are one model, not two. They compute exactly the same
  function and gave identical results. Present it as one circuit:
  - the improvement: fewer feature-map repetitions and ansatz layers. This is
    what actually helps under noise, because it removes gates that sit inside
    the readout's light cone, so less noise reaches qubit 0;
  - then pruning, as an analysis step: it cuts parameters and gate count. But it
    leaves the noisy results unchanged, and with qml.Tracker counting it barely
    changes cost. That's a good "what didn't work, and why" slide.

  For the final runs, keep one of them (pruned, since it has the smaller
  ledger). Running both costs compute and adds nothing.

  2. Don't drop SPSA yet. It's your cost story.
  - The judges explicitly reward "reaching the same performance for less".
  - On seeds 1–3, pruned_spsa is within about 0.04 of pruned for a few percent
    of the executions. Its weak mean comes from seed 0 (0.379), where SPSA got
    stuck early.
  - That's worth one targeted fix before the big run, not abandoning. For
    example, longer patience, or letting the in-script pilot choose its settings
    (the old runs used fixed values).
  - Even if the gap stays, "about 25× cheaper for −0.04 AUPRC" is a valid
    trade-off to present.

  3. Cut or demote the rest, based on evidence:
  - Noise-aware SPSA (_p01, _nat): no gain on any seed so far. On seed 0 they
    never improved on their warm start. Report it as a measured negative result
    unless you want to try a larger fine-tuning step.
  - Class-weight grid (share25, share10): never run on the full data, so there's
    no evidence either way. Run it only if you have the compute, and on
    pruned_spsa, since the weights come out identical and the pruned version is
    cheaper.

  Two cautions before you commit:
  - The evidence is thin. The baseline-vs-improved comparison rests on 2 seeds
    and one noise level (p = 0.05). Wait for all 5 seeds at p = 0.01 and p =
    0.05, where the gap may be smaller.
  - Make the final choice on validation, not test. The brief and the judging
    criteria require that choices come from training and validation data. The
    finished runs only have test numbers (they predate the val_AUPRC column).
    The rerun with the current script will have validation AUPRC, so confirm the
    choice there. That way, "we picked this circuit" is defensible, not
    cherry-picked.

  Also keep the logistic regression next to it in every table. The brief's
  reference point is 0.70 noiseless, and your circuit at about 0.54 under p =
  0.05 should be read against that.

  Plan for the big run: baseline, pruned, pruned_spsa (with the seed-0 fix),
  plus the noise-aware variant and the class-weight grid if time allows. That's
  3–5 models instead of 9, which also fits the 8-colour figures.

  What the results say
  - pruned holds up well under noise. AUPRC goes from 0.556 noiseless to 0.541
    at p = 0.05, ρ stays at 0.98, and the spread across seeds is smallest at the
    highest noise level (± 0.012). F1 and MCC even rise at p = 0.05 (0.66),
    because the threshold is re-chosen on noisy validation scores. It is still
    clearly below the logistic regression (0.70), which is what the brief
    expects at 4 qubits.
  - SPSA trains for 21× fewer executions and loses about 0.05–0.07 AUPRC. The
    gap is mostly seed 0 (0.38 at every noise level); on seeds 1–4 SPSA is at
    0.50–0.55. Its ρ at p = 0.05 is unstable (0.877 ± 0.208), so one SPSA run
    produced a ranking that noise scrambles.
  - Noise-aware SPSA helps the ranking but not AUPRC. At p = 0.05 its AUPRC is
    the same as plain SPSA (0.487 vs 0.487). It does stabilise the ranking: ρ
    goes from 0.877 ± 0.208 to 0.978 ± 0.014. It costs 60% more executions than
    plain SPSA. On seed 0 it kept the warm start, so that seed's weak result is
    inherited, not fixed.

  Costs counted by Tracker equal the formula counts here. The pruned circuit
  already has no skippable parameters, and SPSA uses only forward passes. The
  validation subset cuts validation from 1.71M to 32–52k executions per run,
  which is most of SPSA's saving.

  I fixed a bug in the report while doing this. When it merged several CSVs,
  repeated row numbers let the Tracker fallback overwrite values across files.
  The pruned training count briefly showed 108,800 instead of 407,100. The
  numbers above are after the fix.

  Two things to note:
  - In the cost figure, the labels for _p01 and _nat still overlap a bit,
    because their costs and means are nearly identical.
  - Whether pruned beats the baseline under noise can only be judged when the
    first run finishes. The brief gives the baseline as 0.58 ± 0.05 noiseless,
    and the partial p = 0.05 numbers so far are 0.42 and 0.51 on two seeds. I'll
    add the baseline row as soon as it's done.

❯ I got some more sugestions, now regarding error mitigation.                   
  Setup                                                                         
                                                                                
  Apply mitigation at inference only, on already-trained weights, with no       
  retraining.                                                                   
  Fold the circuit yourself (global folding, U followed by U†U pairs, giving    
  noise scales 1, 3, 5) so the extra gates pass through the official            
  noisy_qnode.                                                                  
  Extrapolate ⟨Z⟩ per sample to zero noise, then convert to a score. Clip the   
  result to [-1, 1].                                                            
  Re-pick the F1 threshold on the mitigated validation scores at each p, as the 
  protocol requires.                                                            
  Add the extra inference executions (validation plus test, times the number of 
  scales) to the cost ledger.                                                   
                                                                                
  Sanity checks                                                                 
  6. At p = 0, folded and unfolded circuits must give identical scores.         
  7. The folded circuit should have about 3× the noise locations at one fold    
  (check with qml.specs).                                                       
                                                                                
  Experiments                                                                   
  8. Scale sets: (1, 3) with linear extrapolation vs (1, 3, 5) with Richardson, 
  at p = 0.01 and 0.05.                                                         
  9. Folding vs “ideal” scaling (the same circuit run at noise s·p). The gap    
  measures how imperfect folding is.                                            
  10. Finite shots (for example 1024) emulated by binomial sampling of each     
  noisy expectation. Exact density matrices hide the variance amplification of  
  extrapolation (about 2.5× for (1, 3), about 5.2× for (1, 3, 5)).              
  11. Which model to mitigate: pruned_spsa first (trained noiselessly, and it   
  had the unstable ρ seed), then pruned_spsa_p01 and pruned_spsa_nat. The last  
  two were fine-tuned on the damped function, so extrapolating to zero noise    
  partly undoes that.                                                           
  12. Optional: an exponential fit on |⟨Z⟩| with the sign kept, falling back to 
  linear when the signs disagree across scales.                                 
                                                                                
  What to compare and report                                                    
  13. Test AUPRC at p = 0.01 and 0.05, Spearman ρ, and F1/MCC, mitigated vs     
  unmitigated, over the same 5 seeds.                                           
  14. Per-seed ρ for pruned_spsa at p = 0.05, to see whether mitigation repairs 
  the collapsed seed.                                                           
  15. Mitigation vs noise-aware fine-tuning as two fixes for the same problem,  
  compared on AUPRC, ρ and ledger executions.                                   
  16. Mitigation vs gate pruning, which is free. If ZNE costs 2–3× inference    
  executions and gains little, report that as a negative result.                
                                                                                
  Caveats for the slides                                                        
  17. The best ZNE can do is return to the noiseless numbers, and your headroom 
  is small (pruned loses about 0.015 AUPRC at p = 0.05).                        
  18. Exact simulation flatters ZNE. State that shot noise would hurt it on     
  real hardware.

● ZNE mitigation is now in main.py, and a quick end-to-end run of --zne worked:
  pilot, training, weight saving, sanity checks, all 9 ZNE variants, the ledger
  and the report, in 51 seconds. I haven't started the full 5-seed experiment,
  because it takes hours (estimate below).

  How it covers the suggestions

  #: setup
  Suggestion: Inference only, no retraining
  Implementation: --zne loads the saved weights from
  weights/<model>_seed<seed>.pt, or trains once and saves them. Normal runs now
   save weights too.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: setup
  Suggestion: Own global folding through the official noisy_qnode
  Implementation: fold_circuit: U followed by (U†U)ᵏ, giving scales 1, 3, 5; the

  folded circuit goes through the unchanged noise model.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: setup
  Suggestion: Extrapolate ⟨Z⟩ per sample, clip to [−1, 1], then score
  Implementation: extrapolate, then to_score
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: setup
  Suggestion: F1 threshold re-chosen on mitigated validation at each p
  Implementation: evaluate_zne, same protocol as evaluate
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: setup
  Suggestion: Extra inference executions in the ledger
  Implementation: New column inference_executions_per_p: (validation + test) ×
  number of scales. That's 113,924 unmitigated, 227,848 for (1,3) and 341,772
  for (1,3,5).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6, 7
  Suggestion: Sanity checks
  Implementation: zne_sanity runs on the first seed of every ZNE run and stops
  the run if a check fails. Both pass: folded vs unfolded agree to ~1e-7 at p =
   0, and noise locations are ×3.00 and ×5.00.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Suggestion: (1,3) linear vs (1,3,5) Richardson
  Implementation: fold13_lin, fold135_rich
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 9
  Suggestion: Folding vs ideal scaling (same circuit at s·p)
  Implementation: ideal13_lin, ideal135_rich
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Suggestion: Finite shots by binomial sampling
  Implementation: shots_none, shots_fold13_lin, shots_fold135_rich. The
  unmitigated row with shots makes the comparison fair. The variance factors
  check out: 2.5 for (1,3) and 5.22 for (1,3,5).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 12
  Suggestion: Exponential fit on |⟨Z⟩| with the sign kept
  Implementation: fold135_exp; it falls back to linear (1,3) when signs differ
  across scales, which I tested.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 13–16
  Suggestion: Mitigated vs unmitigated on the same weights
  Implementation: Each ZNE run includes its own unmitigated rows (/none). The
  report puts them next to pruned, noise-aware SPSA and pruning.

  I fixed one bug along the way. The first version applied the original circuit
  twice and each inverse gate twice, giving 4× the noise locations instead of
  3×. The p = 0 sanity check caught it; it now runs automatically.

  First signal (40 random inputs, untrained weights): ZNE halves to thirds the
  error in ⟨Z⟩ at p = 0.01 (0.083 → 0.026 with Richardson), but barely helps at
  p = 0.05 (0.187 → 0.174; 0.154 with the exponential fit). Folding performs
  like ideal noise scaling. Whether this changes AUPRC or ρ, rather than just
  ⟨Z⟩, is what the full run will show. Your note 17 also applies: pruned only
  loses about 0.015 AUPRC at p = 0.05, so there's little to recover.

  Before running the full experiment:
  1. Time. Each model and seed needs about 22 noisy passes over validation and
     test (114k rows), and the folded circuits are 3× and 5× longer. That's
     roughly 25 minutes per model and seed, so about 2 hours for pruned_spsa,
     pruned_spsa_p01 and pruned_spsa_nat run as 3 parallel processes over 5
     seeds. It will be slower while the first run is still going.
  2. The weights won't be identical to the earlier runs. No weights were saved
     back then, so ZNE retrains, and the SPSA config now has pilot=True, while
     the earlier runs used fixed step sizes. The comparisons within the ZNE run
     are still exact, because /none uses the same weights. To reproduce the
     earlier pruned_spsa numbers exactly, I'd set pilot=False for this run.
  3. Noise levels. ZNE runs at p = 0.01 and 0.05, as the suggestion says
     (ZNE["levels"]). If ZNE ends up as a method you report, the protocol also
     needs p = 0.001. Adding it is one entry, at about +50% run time.

● Correction to something I said earlier: the collapsed-ranking seed for SPSA is
  seed 1 (ρ = 0.505 at p = 0.05), not seed 0. Seed 0 is a different failure:
  SPSA stopped early at step 50, so its AUPRC is low at every noise level.

  The numbers (test set, 5 seeds)

  ┌───────────────┬──────────┬─────────┬─────────┬─────────┬───────────────┐
  │               │ AUPRC, p │  p =    │  p =    │ ρ at p  │ train + val   │
  │               │    = 0   │  0.01   │  0.05   │ = 0.05  │  executions   │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼───────────────┤
  │ logistic      │ 0.703 ±  │ same    │ same    │ 1       │ –             │
  │ regression    │ 0.010    │         │         │         │               │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼───────────────┤
  │ baseline      │ 0.580 ±  │ 0.564 ± │ 0.405 ± │ 0.686 ± │ 2,859,360     │
  │               │ 0.053    │  0.049  │  0.177  │  0.145  │               │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼───────────────┤
  │ pruned        │ 0.556 ±  │ 0.560 ± │ 0.541 ± │ 0.978 ± │ 2,115,960     │
  │               │ 0.039    │  0.031  │  0.012  │  0.014  │               │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼───────────────┤
  │ pruned_spsa   │ 0.488 ±  │ 0.493 ± │ 0.487 ± │ 0.877 ± │ 101,534       │
  │               │ 0.068    │  0.066  │  0.062  │  0.208  │               │
  └───────────────┴──────────┴─────────┴─────────┴─────────┴───────────────┘

  The baseline reproduces the brief's 0.58 ± 0.05, which is a good sign the
  pipeline is set up correctly.

  Conclusions, from strongest to weakest

  1. The smaller circuit makes the VQC robust to noise. This is your main 
  result, and it is solid.
  - From p = 0 to p = 0.05, the baseline loses 0.175 AUPRC and its ranking
    degrades (ρ 0.69). pruned loses 0.015 and keeps ρ at 0.98.
  - At p = 0.05 pruned beats the baseline on all 5 seeds: by +0.131, +0.027,
    +0.054, +0.023 and +0.449.
  - The paired t-test isn't significant (p = 0.16) only because seed 4 is an
    outlier: there the baseline collapses and the gain is +0.449, which inflates
    the variance. A sign test (5 of 5 seeds in the same direction) gives p ≈
    0.06 two-sided.
  - Its spread across seeds also shrinks dramatically, from ± 0.177 to ± 0.012,
    which is good for the "stability across seeds" criterion.
  - Noiseless, pruned is slightly below the baseline (0.556 vs 0.580), but
    that's not significant (p = 0.50). So the gain is robustness, not a better
    model: exactly the "improvement aimed at noise" the brief asks for.
  - What causes it is the smaller architecture (1 feature-map repetition and 2
    ansatz layers instead of 2 and 3). It has fewer gates inside the readout's
    light cone, so less noise reaches qubit 0. The light-cone pruning itself
    contributes nothing, as established before.

  2. SPSA reaches about 90% of pruned's AUPRC for about 5% of the executions. 
  The trade-off is real, but the gap is not precisely measured.
  - The mean gap is −0.05 to −0.07 AUPRC. Seed 0 accounts for much of it:
    training stopped after 50 steps and scored 0.379.
  - Without seed 0 the gap is −0.024 to −0.036. Paired tests give p ≈ 0.12–0.15,
    so with 5 seeds you can't distinguish a small real gap from noise.
  - What you can claim: SPSA is consistently a bit worse, with one clear failure
    mode (early stopping on a noisy validation subset).
  - Against the baseline under noise, SPSA is roughly equal at p = 0.05 (0.487
    vs 0.405, p = 0.40) and worse with little noise (p ≈ 0.01).

  3. Most of the cost saving comes from cheap validation, not from SPSA. This is
  important to state honestly.

  ┌───────────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │               │      training      │    validation     │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ pruned (Adam) │ 407,100            │ 1,708,860         │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ pruned_spsa   │ 69,760 (5.8× less) │ 32,000 (53× less) │
  └───────────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  - Of the ~2.0M executions saved, ~1.68M come from validating on the small
    stratified subset instead of the full set every epoch. SPSA's own
    contribution is the 407k → 70k on training.
  - So a missing ablation is pruned with Adam plus the cheap validation subset.
    It might keep pruned's accuracy at around 15–20% of its cost. If it does,
    that's a better cost story than SPSA. If you present SPSA without this
    ablation, a judge could rightly point it out.

  4. Some results look interesting but don't mean much.
  - F1 and recall rise under noise for pruned (F1 0.611 → 0.660, recall 0.54 →
    0.68) while AUPRC falls slightly. Noise squeezes the scores toward 0.5, the
    validation threshold drops (0.595 → 0.504), and the trade-off between
    precision and recall moves. This is the "single metric reports an
    improvement" trap the brief warns about. Present it as an example of that
    trap, not as a gain.
  - The pruned_spsa ranking collapse on seed 1 (ρ 0.505) didn't hurt AUPRC
    (0.500). ρ is computed over all 57k test transactions, 99.8% of them
    legitimate. The noise reshuffled the order among legitimate transactions
    while the frauds stayed near the top. So the reshuffling that ρ measures
    mostly happens where it doesn't matter.
  - AUPRC at p = 0.01 slightly above p = 0 (0.560 vs 0.556) is noise, not
    regularisation.

  5. Everything stays well below the classical reference. The logistic
  regression on the same 4 angles scores 0.703 ± 0.010 and has no noise. That's
  expected at 4 qubits, and the brief says a quantum result must be read against
  it. Say it plainly on a slide.

  How much the results mean

  - Detectable differences. All seeds share one test set with 98 frauds, so ±
    std reflects training variability only, not test-set sampling. A bootstrap
    over the test set adds roughly ± 0.03–0.05 to each AUPRC. Differences under
    ~0.03 (SPSA vs pruned without seed 0, pruned vs baseline noiseless) are
    within noise. The baseline-vs-pruned gap at p = 0.05 and the gaps in ρ are
    well beyond it.
  - 5 seeds is the minimum the brief requires. It's enough for the large effect
    (robustness at p = 0.05), not for small ones.
  - Selection. These runs have no val_AUPRC (they predate the column). The rerun
    should confirm on validation that pruned is the circuit you'd pick, so the
    choice isn't made on test.

  What would strengthen the conclusions cheaply

  1. pruned with Adam plus cheap validation. This attributes the cost saving
     correctly.
  2. SPSA with longer patience, or with the pilot. This fixes the seed-0 early
     stop and shows whether the gap is mostly that failure.
  3. Bootstrap confidence intervals on test AUPRC. This makes "within noise"
     explicit on the slides (the notebook check had them).
  4. Optionally, 10 seeds for SPSA. It's cheap, around 100k executions per run,
     and tightens the estimate of the gap.
