  ⎿  … 15 more files changed

● I implemented all four points in main.py and tested them end to end in quick
  mode with 2 seeds. Nothing long has run yet; the real experiments need your
  go-ahead (plan below).

  1. Adam with cheap validation (pruned_cheapval)
  - New val_subset=True option for Adam training. Each epoch it validates on the
    same stratified subset SPSA uses (all frauds plus 5% of legitimate
    transactions, weighted AUPRC) instead of the full 56,962 rows. Training
    itself is unchanged: 30 epochs of exact gradients.
  - The ledger counts what it actually spent, under both the formula and the
    Tracker count.
  - This model answers how much of the saving comes from cheap validation alone.
    Expected cost: about 407k training + ~88k validation, so ~17% of pruned,
    versus SPSA's ~5%.

  2. SPSA without early stopping (pruned_spsa_full)
  - Same as pruned_spsa, but with patience disabled. It runs all 600 steps and
    keeps the best checkpoint by validation subset.
  - This tests whether seed 0's failure (stopping at step 50) explains most of
    SPSA's gap.

  3. Bootstrap confidence intervals
  - Every run now saves its test scores to {out}_scores.npz.
  - The report resamples the test set 200 times by default (--bootstrap N).
    Frauds and legitimate rows are resampled separately, and all models are
    scored on the same resamples.
  - For each model at p = 0.01 and 0.05 it reports:
    - the mean AUPRC over seeds with a 95% CI;
    - the paired difference from a reference model (--reference, default
      baseline), computed only on the seeds both have, with its 95% CI;
    - the share of resamples in which the model wins.
  - Output: {out}_final_bootstrap.csv. It captures the uncertainty from the ~98
    test frauds, which the spread over seeds doesn't see.

  4. More seeds (--seeds 0-9)
  - The official SEEDS stays at 5. --seeds overrides it for ablations only, and
    paired comparisons use the seeds both models share.

  Proposed run. It's one process per model at 4 threads each, 20 threads in
  total, which is your core count:

  ┌─────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ process │    models / seeds    │                  why                   │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │         │                      │ its test scores are needed for the     │
  │ 1       │ baseline, seeds 0–4  │ bootstrap; the slowest job (heavy      │
  │         │                      │ noisy evaluation)                      │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2       │ pruned, seeds 0–9    │ the reference for the SPSA comparisons │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3       │ pruned_cheapval,     │ ablation 1                             │
  │         │ seeds 0–4            │                                        │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4       │ pruned_spsa, seeds   │ 10-seed estimate of the gap            │
  │         │ 0–9                  │                                        │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 5       │ pruned_spsa_full,    │ ablation 2                             │
  │         │ seeds 0–9            │                                        │
  └─────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  - I expect roughly 2–3 hours, with the baseline the limiting job.
  - Then two report calls:
    - --reference baseline: is the robustness gain real, beyond test noise?
    - --reference pruned: how large is SPSA's gap, and does cheap validation
      cost anything?
  - Weights are saved this time, so ZNE can reuse them later without retraining.

  Two notes:
  - All SPSA models will run with the pilot (pilot=True), unlike the earlier
    runs. So pruned_spsa won't reproduce its previous numbers exactly. The new
    ones are the defensible ones, since the step sizes are then chosen on
    validation inside the script.
  - The new CSVs will have val_AUPRC, so you can also confirm the model choice
    on validation.

  Shall I launch it?
✻ Crunched for 2m 40s · done Saturday 18:15

❯ Can you think of any ways to increase the AUPRC scores of the model?          

● Yes. The most useful clue is in your own results: logistic regression on the 
  same 4 angles reaches 0.70, the VQC 0.56–0.58. So the information is in the
  inputs. What's missing is the circuit's ability to turn it into a good
  ranking, or the training's ability to find that. That points to ideas in
  roughly this order (the cheapest with the most likely gain first):

  1. Replace the ZZ feature map with a simple angle encoding. encoding="angle"
  is already in make_circuit.
  - The ZZ map uses RZ(2x) and products 2(π−xᵢ)(π−xⱼ), which reach about 20
    radians. That makes the model a fast-oscillating function of the inputs,
    while what works here (LR) is a smooth, almost monotone one.
  - RY(x) with x in [0, π] gives ⟨Z⟩ = cos x, which is monotone: much closer to
    what LR exploits.
  - It also removes all the feature-map CNOTs, so it helps under noise too.
  - This is my strongest hypothesis, and testing it is just a new config.

  2. Train on the full training set with class weights, not 590 rows.
  - Adam currently sees only 295 legitimate transactions. Precision at high
    recall is decided by the hard legitimate ones, the few that look like fraud,
    and the model barely sees them.
  - The StratifiedSampler already exists (SPSA uses it). Feeding Adam the same
    batches (half frauds, weighted) gives it 170k negatives to learn from. The
    brief lists this explicitly ("class weights instead of undersampling").
  - Then sweep loss_pos_share over 0.5, 0.25 and 0.1, as planned.

  3. Classical rescaling of the input and the output (the rules allow both:
  "classical layers may prepare the inputs or rescale the circuit's output").
  - Input: a trainable scale and offset per feature, RY(aᵢ·xᵢ + bᵢ). The model
    can then choose how fast it varies with each feature instead of being stuck
    with [0, π]. It adds 8 classical parameters and zero gates.
  - Output: σ(α⟨Z⟩ + β) instead of (1 − ⟨Z⟩)/2. Ranking is the same, but BCE
    gets sharper probabilities and larger gradients, which usually helps the
    optimiser find a better ranking. It also undoes the compression noise
    causes.

  4. Data re-uploading (Pérez-Salinas, cited in the brief).
  - Alternate encoding and trainable layers 2–3 times. That gives a much richer
    function class (Fourier series in the inputs) on the same 4 qubits.
  - It costs gates, so it trades directly against noise. Combined with idea 1
    (angle encoding, no CNOTs in the encoding), it stays cheap.

  5. Better inputs: select features instead of using PCA.
  - PCA keeps the directions of largest variance, not those that best separate
    fraud. A few V-features (V14, V17, V12, V10) are known to be strongly
    discriminative on this dataset.
  - Choose them on training data only, for example by mutual information. Also
    replace MinMax + clipping (outliers pile up at 0 or π) with a quantile or
    arctan squashing.
  - This changes the logistic regression too, since it must use the same inputs.
    Both should rise, so present it as a pipeline improvement, not a quantum
    one.

  6. A loss closer to the metric. BCE optimises calibration on a balanced
  distribution, while AUPRC only rewards ranking frauds above the highest-scored
  legitimate transactions. A pairwise ranking loss (each fraud vs the hardest
  negatives in the batch) or focal loss optimises that directly. Moderate
  effort, and it would count as original.

  7. Training details.
  - The best epochs fall between 1 and 26 with lr = 0.05, and validation is
    noisy. A lower learning rate with cosine decay, more epochs (cheap with
    cheap validation), and choosing epochs or hyperparameters on validation
    would likely help a little.
  - An ensemble over seeds (averaging scores) cuts the variance but multiplies
    the cost, so it's a different method with its own ledger.

  8. Readout from more than one qubit. For example a trainable weighted sum of
  ⟨Zᵢ⟩, or a parity like ⟨Z₀Z₁⟩. It adds expressivity without gates, but it's
  borderline under the rule that "the classification must come from the
  circuit", and more qubits pick up noise.

  What I'd do first: 1, 2 and 3 together. They're the cheapest, most likely to
  close part of the gap to LR, and 1 and 3 add no gates, so they don't sacrifice
  your noise robustness.

  Two cautions:
  - Every added gate costs AUPRC at p = 0.05, and robustness is your strongest
    result. Check each idea at p = 0.01 and 0.05, not only noiseless.
  - Choose on validation, and read gains against the bootstrap. Differences
    under about 0.03 are within the test-set noise.

  If you want, I can add these as models (angle, angle_fullweights,
  angle_rescaled and so on) to the planned ablation run, so they're measured
  with the same 5 seeds, ledger and bootstrap.

✻ Churned for 41s · done Saturday 18:20

❯ What points do you think would add the most to the score?                     

● I'll read "the score" as the jury's overall score, using the judging criteria
  at the end of the brief. If you meant AUPRC itself, the first two points below
  are also the AUPRC answer.

  Ranked by expected gain for the time it takes:

  1. Angle encoding instead of the ZZ feature map: the highest-value single 
  change.
  - Performance under noise: fewer gates means less noise. It has a real chance
    of closing part of the gap to the logistic regression.
  - Circuit quality: it reduces depth, which the brief rewards explicitly
    ("circuits that reduce qubit count or depth").
  - Research thinking: you can state it as a testable hypothesis. "The logistic
    regression reaches 0.70 on the same 4 angles, so the information is there.
    The ZZ map makes the model oscillate (angles up to about 20 rad), while the
    target is nearly monotone, and RY(x) gives cos x, which is monotone."
  - It's already in the code (encoding="angle"), so it costs only a config line
    and a run.

  2. The planned ablation run with bootstrap intervals: not a new idea, but what
  makes everything else count.
  - It covers several criteria at once: sound experiment design
    (validation-based choices, CIs on test), stability across seeds (10 seeds
    for SPSA), research thinking (ablations), and cost (correctly attributing
    the saving to cheap validation vs SPSA).
  - Right now your strongest result (pruned beats the baseline at p = 0.05 on 5
    of 5 seeds) has no confidence interval. The cheap-validation ablation also
    protects you from the obvious jury question about where the SPSA saving
    really comes from.
  - I'd include idea 1's models in this same run.

  3. Full training data with class weights.
  - The brief lists it as a direction. It probably raises AUPRC, since the model
    then sees the hard legitimate cases that decide precision. It also fits
    your cost story: SPSA already trains this way, so you'd test it for Adam too
    and include the loss_pos_share grid.
  - Medium effort: Adam just needs to use the existing sampler.

  4. Your honest negative results, presented well. These cost nothing more to 
  compute. The brief says "an honestly measured negative result is a result",
  and the jury scores "understanding of the problem". You already have three
  good ones:
  - light-cone pruning removes noise locations but changes nothing under this
    noise model, and you can explain exactly why;
  - noise-aware SPSA stabilises ρ but doesn't improve AUPRC;
  - F1 rising under noise while AUPRC falls is the trap the brief warns about.

  This is probably the cheapest way to add points, since it's slide work, not
  compute.

  5. Input and output rescaling. Cheap, adds no gates, and is allowed by the
  rules. A probable small gain; worth adding to the same run as a variant of
  idea 1. Low originality on its own.

  Lower priority, given the time:
  - ZNE. It's implemented, but the full run takes hours and the headroom is
    small (pruned loses only about 0.015 at p = 0.05). If you have spare compute
    at the end, one model at p = 0.01 and 0.05 makes a nice cost-vs-gain slide
    (likely a negative result).
  - Pairwise ranking loss. It would score on originality, but it's risky to
    debug in the time left.
  - Feature selection instead of PCA. It raises the logistic regression too, so
    it doesn't improve the quantum-vs-classical comparison the jury looks at.
  - Data re-uploading, more qubits, multi-qubit readout. These cost gates (and
    so noise), or brush against the "classification must come from the circuit"
    rule.

  My suggestion: add angle, angle with class weights, and angle with rescaling
  as models to the planned ablation run, and launch everything together. Write
  the slides with the negative results while it runs.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Angle encoding is in main.py, and the runs have started: five processes, 5
  seeds each, 4 threads each (20 cores in total).

  What I changed
  - New models:
    - angle_pruned: Adam;
    - angle_pruned_spsa: SPSA with the pilot;
    - angle_pruned_r0: an ablation with readout on qubit 0.
    - All three use RY(xᵢ) per qubit instead of the ZZ feature map, with pruning
      and merging on.
  - I kept the ZZ models as the comparison, so the hypothesis ("the ZZ map, not
    the inputs, limits the VQC") can be tested directly.
  - The angle encoding is now written as individual RY gates (equivalent to
    qml.AngleEmbedding), so pruning can drop them one by one.
  - The Tracker count now runs example by example. After merging, a gate can
    combine data and weights, which parameter-shift can't handle in a batch. The
    earlier circuits' counts are unchanged.

  Two findings from building it
  1. With readout on qubit 0, the 4th feature (PC4) never reaches the output.
     The CNOT chain goes 0→1→2→3, and two layers aren't enough to carry
     information back from qubit 3. The ZZ map hid this because its all-to-all
     CNOTs mixed every feature in. Reading out qubit 1 reaches all 4 inputs.
  2. Merging finally helps. RY(xᵢ) followed by the trainable RY(wᵢ) becomes one
     gate.

  ┌────────────────────────────┬───────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────┐

  The angle circuit has less than half the noise of the pruned ZZ one, and a
  fifth of the baseline's. It costs a bit more to train per example (15
  parameters instead of 11).

● The angle-encoding hypothesis is confirmed, and by a wide margin.

  What this means
  - Angle encoding gains about +0.11 AUPRC noiseless and +0.26 at p = 0.05 over 
    the baseline. angle_pruned beats the baseline on every seed at both headline
    noise levels (e.g. 0.660 vs 0.421 and 0.679 vs 0.097 at p = 0.05). It also
    beats ZZ pruned on every seed.
  - It is almost immune to the noise model. AUPRC goes from 0.671 to 0.669
    between p = 0 and p = 0.05, ρ stays at 0.99, and the spread across seeds is
    ± 0.008, the smallest of any model. That fits 25 noise locations vs 126 for
    the baseline.
  - The gap to the classical reference shrinks from 0.12 to 0.03. F1 at the
    validation threshold matches the logistic regression's (0.790 vs 0.791).
    Whether the remaining 0.03 AUPRC is real or test noise is for the bootstrap
    to say.
  - SPSA now nearly matches Adam: 0.662 vs 0.669 at p = 0.05, for 3.2% of the 
    baseline's executions. With ZZ the gap was about 0.05. A smoother model is
    easier for a noisy gradient estimate to optimise. That makes your cost story
    much stronger.
  - PC4 is worth its gates. Reading out qubit 0 (no PC4) scores 0.656 ± 0.022
    against 0.669 ± 0.008. Validation agrees (0.642 vs 0.667 at p = 0.05), so
    choosing readout 1 is defensible without looking at test.
  - Validation tracks test closely (0.668 vs 0.671 for angle_pruned), so model
    selection on validation works here.

  Still running: the ZZ reruns, pruned and pruned_spsa, are on seed 3 of 5. The
  first seeds of the ZZ pruned_spsa rerun look worse than before (0.411, 0.512,
  0.213 at p = 0.05). Its pilot chose a smaller step (0.01 instead of the
  earlier fixed 0.03), which suits ZZ badly; the same pilot picked well for the
  angle model. That's worth a line on a slide: SPSA's sensitivity to its
  settings depends on the circuit. When they finish I'll run the bootstrap with
  pruned as the reference. That puts confidence intervals on "angle beats ZZ"
  and on the remaining gap to the logistic regression.

  Conclusions you can defend:
  1. Angle encoding is the main improvement. It beats the ZZ circuit by about
     0.12 AUPRC at both headline noise levels, with intervals clear of zero.
     It's chosen on validation too: it has the best validation AUPRC at p = 0.01
     and 0.05. Against the baseline at p = 0.05 it's 0.669 vs 0.405.
  2. It's barely affected by noise. AUPRC goes 0.671 → 0.669 from p = 0 to 0.05,
     ρ stays at 0.99, and the spread across seeds is ± 0.008. That matches its
     25 noise locations, against 126 for the baseline.
  3. SPSA reaches the same accuracy for 3.2% of the baseline's executions. With
     angle encoding, SPSA isn't measurably worse than Adam (the interval
     covers 0). That's your best cost claim: same performance, 31× cheaper.
  4. The VQC now sits 0.03 below the logistic regression. The gap is small but
     statistically real. Present it honestly: the quantum model closed most of
     the gap but doesn't beat the classical reference on the same inputs.

  New negative result: with ZZ encoding, the in-script pilot picked a step size
  that made SPSA worse and unstable: 0.424 ± 0.127, against 0.487 with the
  earlier fixed values. With angle encoding the same pilot works well. SPSA's
  sensitivity to its settings depends on the circuit, as Kübler et al. warned.
  Slide-worthy.

  For the final submission, two pieces are missing:
  - The baseline needs a rerun with the current code. The earlier run has no
    saved scores or val_AUPRC, so the bootstrap can't compare against it yet.
    The brief makes the baseline the required reference, so this is the one
    rerun that really matters. It's the slowest model, roughly 1–1.5 hours on
    its own.
  - The cheap-validation ablation (pruned_cheapval, ideally an angle version
    too) and 10 seeds for angle_pruned_spsa would complete the cost story. Both
    are cheap, since the angle circuits evaluate quickly.

  Shall I launch the baseline rerun, plus the angle versions of these two
  ablations, in parallel?

※ recap: You're building a cheaper, noise-robust VQC for the challenge, and angle encoding (0.669 AUPRC at p = 0.05) plus SPSA at 3% of the baseline's cost are the current best results. Next: rerun the baseline with the current code so it can be compared with confidence intervals.

❯ What other improvements can I add to increase the AUPRC score?                

● Since the last answer, angle encoding brought you to 0.67, and the logistic
  regression on the same 4 inputs is at 0.70. So the question splits into two:
  - what lifts the VQC itself;
  - what lifts everything, including the classical reference.

  Ranked by expected gain for the effort:

  Lifts the VQC itself (closes the gap to the logistic regression)

  1. Trainable input scaling: RY(aᵢ·xᵢ + bᵢ). With plain RY(xᵢ), each feature
     enters as a cos/sin of the raw angle, a fixed shape. A scale and offset per
     feature let the model choose how steep each feature's effect is and where
     it sits. That's exactly the freedom a logistic regression has with its
     weights.
     - 8 classical parameters, zero extra gates (the offset merges into the next
       RY). The brief allows it ("classical layers may prepare the inputs").
     - My top pick: cheap, noise-free, and aimed directly at the gap.
  2. Full training data with class weights, instead of 590 rows. Adam currently
     sees only 295 legitimate transactions. Precision at high recall depends on
     the few legitimate ones that look like fraud, and the model almost never
     sees them.
     - Train on all 170k with the existing stratified sampler (half frauds per
       batch, weighted loss), plus the loss_pos_share grid 0.5 / 0.25 / 0.1.
     - No extra gates. More training executions per epoch, but SPSA already
       trains this way at low cost.
  3. Output rescaling σ(α⟨Z⟩ + β). The ranking is unchanged, but the loss sees
     sharper probabilities, so gradients stay informative near the decision
     boundary. Two classical parameters, no gates. A small, cheap gain; it
     combines well with ideas 1 and 2.
  4. Training settings, chosen on validation. Several seeds picked epoch 1 as
     best, which suggests lr = 0.05 overshoots and the noisy validation then
     picks an early checkpoint.
     - Try a lower learning rate with cosine decay and more epochs. More epochs
       are cheap with the validation subset.
     - No gates; just a small grid.
  5. Data re-uploading (two encoding layers). Encoding the inputs twice, with a
     trainable layer between, lets the model represent products and second
     harmonics of the features, things a logistic regression can't.
     - The only idea here that could plausibly beat the logistic regression, but
       it adds about 4 gates and 4 CNOTs.
     - Your circuit is so robust (25 noise locations, almost no loss at p =
       0.05) that there's room to spend a few gates.

  Lifts everything (VQC and logistic regression together)

  6. Better angle scaling. The MinMaxScaler is fitted on 590 rows, so test
     transactions outside that range are clipped to exactly 0 or π. Frauds are
     often the extremes, so their ordering is lost.
     - A quantile or arctan transform (fitted on training data only) keeps the
       ordering.
     - Both models improve, which helps the absolute score but not the
       quantum-vs-classical comparison.
  7. More qubits (5–6 principal components). The angle circuit is small, so one
     or two more qubits stay affordable under noise (25 → ~35 noise locations).
     Simulation gets about 4× slower per qubit, and the brief suggests staying
     at 6 or fewer. The logistic regression gets the extra features too.
  8. Feature selection instead of PCA. Choose the most fraud-discriminative
     features (e.g. by mutual information on training data) rather than the
     highest-variance directions. Likely the biggest absolute gain, but it lifts
     the logistic regression just as much.

  What I'd do: ideas 1 + 3 together (input and output scaling, no gates), plus 2
  (full data), and if time allows 5 (re-uploading) as the "could beat the
  classical reference" attempt. Ideas 6–8 are worth it if you want the highest
  absolute AUPRC, with the caveat that the classical reference rises too.

  As before: choose on val_AUPRC, check p = 0.05 so extra gates don't eat your
  robustness, and read gains against the bootstrap. Differences under about
  0.02–0.03 are within the test noise.

  Want me to add 1 + 3, 2 and 5 as new angle models and run them, together with
  the baseline rerun the submission still needs?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● I've added input and output scaling to main.py, and the four fast runs have
  started (5 seeds each, 5 threads each).

  What's implemented
  - Input scaling (input_scaling=True): each feature is encoded as RY(aᵢ·xᵢ + 
    bᵢ), with a trainable scale aᵢ and offset bᵢ per qubit. They start at
    (1, 0), so training starts from exactly the plain angle model. Merging folds
    the offset into the following trainable RY, so there are no extra gates or 
    noise locations (still 20 gates and 25 noise locations).
  - Output scaling (output_scaling=True): the loss uses sigmoid(−α⟨Z⟩ + β), with
    α kept positive and starting at 2.
    - It is a strictly increasing function of the usual score, so ranking,
      AUPRC, Spearman ρ and the metrics at the validation threshold mean the
      same thing as before. Evaluation still uses the plain score.
    - Only what the model learns changes, not how it's measured.
  - Where the extra values live: the classical parameters are extra rows in the
    weights tensor. That way Adam, SPSA, saving and loading, and the cost ledger
    handle them with no special cases. They cost no circuit executions, so the
    Tracker count stays 31 per example.
  - Allowed by the rules: classical layers prepare the inputs and rescale the
    output; the classification still comes from the circuit.

  Running now (output files task2_v3_<model>_*):

  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ angle_outscaled (output only) │ 0.670         │ 0.660                   │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ angle_scaled_spsa             │ 0.652         │ 0.635                   │
  │                               │               │ (angle_pruned_spsa)     │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ logistic regression           │ 0.699 on this │                         │
  │ (reference)                   │  seed         │                         │
  └───────────────────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  What it hints at:
  - Input scaling looks like the useful part. On this seed it lifts the model by
    about 0.04 and reaches the logistic regression's level (0.704 vs 0.699).
    Output scaling alone gains much less (+0.01).
  - Combining both is slightly below input scaling alone on this seed. That's
    within the seed-to-seed noise, so no conclusion yet.
  - SPSA also improves a little with scaling (0.652 vs 0.635).

  These are single-seed numbers, and the test-set noise alone is around ±
  0.02–0.03, so treat them as a direction, not a result. The 5-seed means, the
  validation AUPRC for choosing between variants, and the paired bootstrap
  against angle_pruned and the logistic regression will settle it. I'll run that
  report as soon as all four finish.

  The picture has changed since seed 0:
  - Both scalings together look best, +0.016 over plain angle encoding on
    average, and better on 3 of 4 seeds. That halves the gap to the logistic
    regression (0.701), from about 0.034 to about 0.018.
  - Input scaling alone is the most unstable. It's the best of all on seed 0
    (0.704) but the worst on seed 2 (0.631). Its mean gain is small (+0.003).
    Output scaling seems to stabilise it, which fits: input scaling makes
    training more flexible, and the sharper output gives it better gradients.
  - Output scaling alone is slightly worse (−0.014).
  - SPSA doesn't benefit from scaling (0.647 vs 0.657). The extra parameters
    probably make SPSA's noisy gradient estimates harder to use, and the pilot
    found the same step size as before (0.01). For the cheap-training story,
    plain angle_pruned_spsa is still the better choice.

  How much these mean: the differences are 0.01–0.02 on four seeds. That's about
  the size of the test-set noise we measured before (± 0.02–0.03 on paired
  differences), so none of it is established yet. Several seeds again picked
  epoch 1–2 as best, which supports lowering the learning rate as the next
  tweak.

  When seed 4 finishes (a few minutes), I'll run the full report: all noise
  levels, val_AUPRC to make the choice on validation, and the paired bootstrap
  against angle_pruned and the logistic regression. That will say whether
  angle_scaled's gain is real.

  All scaled models keep the same circuit (20 gates, 25 noise locations) and
  stay almost unaffected by noise (ρ ≥ 0.993 at p = 0.05).

  What it means
  1. Both scalings together give the best mean, but the gain isn't established.
     +0.010 at p = 0.05, with an interval that includes 0, and better on 4 of 5
     seeds. Validation picks it too at p = 0.05 (0.675 vs 0.667), so choosing it
     is defensible without looking at test. If the gain is real, the gap to the
     logistic regression shrinks from 0.030 to about 0.02. But it is still a
     gap.
  2. The trade-off is stability. angle_scaled varies more across seeds (± 0.016
     vs ± 0.008). Input scaling alone is the most unstable (± 0.029: best seed
     0.704, worst 0.631). The judges score stability across seeds, so plain
     angle_pruned remains a strong alternative: almost the same AUPRC, half the
     spread.
  3. Output scaling alone is measurably worse (−0.021, interval clear of 0).
     That's a real negative result. A plausible reason: the sharper
     probabilities make Adam fit the 590 training rows faster. Several seeds
     picked epoch 1 as best, so the early checkpoint ends up worse. Combined
     with input scaling, the extra flexibility of the input apparently balances
     it out.
  4. SPSA gets worse with scaling (−0.030, significant). Ten classical
     parameters with very different sensitivities make SPSA's single
     random-direction step harder to use. For the cheap-training result, keep
     angle_pruned_spsa: 0.662 for 92k executions, not measurably below Adam.

  For the presentation:
  - Main model: angle_scaled, picked on validation, with the honest caveat that
    its gain over plain angle encoding (+0.01) is within test noise. Or present
    angle_pruned as the main model and scaling as an ablation; both are
    defensible.
  - Cheap training: angle_pruned_spsa.
  - Negative results: output scaling alone; scaling + SPSA; ZZ + the pilot.

  Still missing for the submission:
  1. The baseline rerun with the current code, so the final table and bootstrap
     can be against the required baseline. The earlier baseline run lacks saved
     scores and val_AUPRC. This is the one remaining must-have, about 1–1.5
     hours.
  2. Optional: a lower learning rate for angle_scaled, since several seeds
     picked epoch 1–2 as best, which suggests the training overshoots. It's
     cheap with these small circuits.

  Should I launch the baseline rerun now, and the learning-rate test alongside
  it?

  What the numbers say
  1. The Adam angle models are not cheaper; they're better. They cost 93% of the
     baseline by Tracker count. Training is cheaper (548,700 vs 725,700), but
     validation dominates: 1.71M of their 2.26M executions (76%) go to scoring
     the full validation set every epoch. So for these models the gain is
     accuracy and robustness, not cost.
  2. angle_pruned_spsa is the cost result. 3.8% of the baseline under the
     stricter Tracker count, with AUPRC at p = 0.05 of 0.662 against the
     baseline's 0.405, and not measurably below Adam's 0.669. That's "better
     performance for 26× less".
  3. Count the pilot honestly. The one-off pilot (172k) costs more than a whole
     SPSA training run. Spread over the 5 seeds it adds about 34k per run. That
     gives about 126k per run, about 5% of the baseline: still a ~20× saving.
     The total for all 5 seeds: 5 × 92k + 172k = 633k, against 5 × 2.43M = 12.2M
     for the baseline.
  4. Inference is the same for every model: 113,924 executions (validation +
     test) per noise level. It isn't part of the training ledger. It would only
     grow with ZNE (×2 or ×3).

  The cheap fix this points to. The Adam angle models' cost is mostly
  validation, and the cheap-validation ablation is already in the code
  (val_subset=True). Projected by formula:
  - angle_pruned with cheap validation: 548,700 + 30 × 2,943 ≈ 637k (26% of the 
    Tracker baseline), presumably with similar accuracy. That's the same
    question pruned_cheapval was meant to answer.
  - Several seeds picked epoch 1–2 as best, so fewer epochs probably lose
    little. 10 epochs with cheap validation would be about 212k (≈ 9%).

  That would give two points on the cost curve: Adam at about 9–26% of the
  baseline, and SPSA at about 4–5%. Each angle run takes about 15–20 minutes.
  Should I add angle_scaled with cheap validation (30 and 10 epochs) to the next
  batch, next to the baseline rerun?

  Read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

● The precision-recall curves are saved as two figures in the project folder:
  - task2_v3_main_pr.png: the main models at p = 0.01 and p = 0.05;
  - task2_v3_ablations_pr.png: the scaling ablations at p = 0.05.

  Each curve is the mean over 5 seeds, with a band from the worst to the best
  seed, and the logistic regression is the dashed line. plot_pr_curves is now
  part of main.py, and the report produces it automatically from the saved
  scores.

  I fixed a bug first. My first version drew every curve flat at precision 1. I
  had computed the "best precision at recall ≥ r" from the wrong end of
  sklearn's arrays. Now the area under each curve is consistent with its AUPRC
  (slightly above it, as interpolation always is).

  What the curves show

  1. Every angle model and the logistic regression have the same shape.
     Precision stays around 0.83 from about 10% to 78% recall, then collapses at
     about 80% recall. So roughly a fifth of the test frauds look like
     legitimate transactions in these 4 features, and no model, quantum or
     classical, catches them without a flood of false alarms. That's a limit of
     the 4-angle inputs, not of the circuit.
  2. The 0.03 gap to the logistic regression comes almost entirely from the very
     top of the ranking. Its first ~17% of frauds come with precision 1.0: its
     highest-scored transactions are all fraud. The VQCs mix a few legitimate
     transactions into their very top scores, so precision dips to about 0.84
     already at 5–10% recall. Across the middle plateau, angle_scaled is
     actually slightly above the logistic regression (about 0.84 vs 0.81).
     - A hypothesis worth a slide: with RY(x), the output depends on cos x,
       which flattens near 0 and π. The most extreme transactions get compressed
       scores, and some of them are clipped to exactly π by the MinMax scaler.
       So the circuit can't order the extremes, where many frauds sit, while the
       linear logistic regression can.
     - This points directly at the earlier idea of an arctan or quantile
       transform instead of MinMax with clipping, or a narrower angle range.
  3. Angle vs ZZ (orange): the ZZ circuit's plateau is lower (about 0.78) and
     starts falling at about 55–65% recall, 15–20 points earlier. That's the
     AUPRC difference made visible.
  4. Noise barely changes the angle curves. The p = 0.01 and 0.05 panels are
     almost identical. ZZ pruned shifts visibly between them.
  5. SPSA (green) follows Adam closely at p = 0.05. At p = 0.01 its band is wide
     between 55% and 80% recall: one or two seeds fall off earlier, the same
     seed instability seen in the AUPRC spread.
  6. Ablations: angle_outscaled and angle_scaled_spsa fall off earlier (from
     about 60–75% recall), which matches their significantly lower AUPRC.
     angle_inscaled and angle_scaled sit on top of the plateau with
     angle_pruned. Their small gains are mostly in the first 10–20% of recall.

  So the curves show where the VQC loses to the classical model and suggest a
  concrete fix: better angle scaling at the extremes. If you want to try it,
  it's a preprocessing change plus one fast angle run, and the logistic
  regression gets the same inputs, so the comparison stays fair.

❯ Any other improvements we could try?                                          

● Yes. The precision-recall curves now point at two specific weaknesses, so the
  most promising ideas are the ones that target them.

  Weakness 1: legitimate transactions mixed into the highest scores (where the
  0.03 gap to the logistic regression comes from)

  1. Train on the full training set with class weights. The legitimate
     transactions that end up in the VQC's top scores are hard negatives: they
     look like fraud. The model has seen only 295 legitimate transactions in
     training, so it has had almost no chance to learn to push those down.
     Training on all 170k (half-fraud batches, weighted loss, with the existing
     sampler), plus the loss_pos_share grid, aims straight at this. No extra
     gates. My first pick.
  2. Fix the angle scaling at the extremes. cos x flattens near 0 and π, and the
     MinMax scaler is fitted on only 590 rows, so many test transactions are
     clipped to exactly 0 or π and can't be ordered. Options, all fitted on
     training data only:
     - fit the scaler on the full training set (fewer clipped test values);
     - an arctan or quantile transform (keeps the order of outliers);
     - a narrower range such as [0.1π, 0.9π].

     It's a one-line preprocessing change. The logistic regression gets the same
     inputs, so the comparison stays fair.

  Weakness 2: the cliff at about 80% recall (frauds that look legitimate in 4
  features)

  3. More qubits, i.e. 5–6 principal components. No model separates the last
     ~20% of frauds with these 4 features, quantum or classical, so the limit is
     the inputs. Each extra qubit adds only about 6 noise locations to the
     angle circuit, which is cheap given how robust it is, while simulation gets
     about 4× slower per qubit. The logistic regression gains the same
     features. Biggest possible absolute gain, but probably not a better
     quantum-vs-classical comparison.

  Training stability

  4. Lower learning rate, more epochs. Several seeds still choose epoch 1–2 as
     best, which suggests lr = 0.05 overshoots and the noisy validation grabs an
     early checkpoint. A small grid (lr 0.01 or 0.02, with cosine decay),
     chosen on validation, might cut the seed spread and add a little AUPRC.
     Cheap.

  Bigger bets

  5. Data re-uploading. Two encoding layers give the circuit second harmonics
     and feature interactions that the logistic regression can't express. It's
     the only idea here that could plausibly beat the classical reference. It
     adds about 4 gates + 3 CNOTs, which the circuit's noise margin can absorb.
  6. A ranking loss focused on the top. Penalise each fraud scored below the
     highest-scored legitimate transactions in its batch. It targets weakness 1
     directly and would count as original, but it's riskier to tune in the time
     left.

  Order I'd try them in: 1 + 2 + 4 together in one batch of fast angle runs
  (about 20 minutes, all gate-free changes), then 5 if there's time. Idea 3 is
  worth a run if you care more about absolute AUPRC than about the comparison
  with the logistic regression.

  One more reminder: the baseline rerun with the current code is still the only
  must-have left for the submission. It can run alongside these.

  Should I implement 1, 2 and 4 as new angle models and launch them together
  with the baseline rerun?

● I implemented the three improvements in main.py and started the runs: five
  angle variants plus the baseline rerun, all 5 seeds.

  What's new
  1. Full training data with class weights (full_data=True). Adam trains on all
     170k training rows with stratified batches: half fraud, with loss weights
     so the loss still matches the balanced objective.
     - Each epoch has 19 batches of 32, about 590 examples, so the cost per
       epoch matches the baseline. But over the run the model sees thousands of
       different legitimate transactions instead of the same 295.
     - The weighting is set by loss_pos_share (0.5 and 0.25 here).
  2. Arctan angle mapping (squash="arctan"): angle = π/2 + arctan(z), with z
     each component standardised on training data. It's strictly monotone with
     no clipping. The original mapping is unchanged, as verified.
  3. Lower learning rate with cosine decay (learning_rate=0.02,
     lr_schedule="cosine"). The learning rate falls smoothly to 0 over the 30
     epochs.

  A finding from checking fix 2: the current MinMax mapping clips practically no
  test values, and only about 2% of frauds have a single clipped feature. So
  clipping isn't what costs the VQC at the top of the ranking. That leaves the
  flatness of cos near 0 and π as the possible culprit. Arctan tests that,
  though it could just as well hurt, since it compresses the extremes further. I
  dropped the "MinMax fitted on all training data" variant because it changed
  nothing measurable.

  Running now (output files task2_v4_<model>_*):

  What it looks like so far:
  - Full training data is the only change that helps, and only a little
    (+0.007). It is the best or near-best on 3 of 4 seeds. That's in line with
    the hard-negatives explanation, but too small to call yet.
  - Arctan hurts clearly (−0.050, very unstable: 0.711 down to 0.568). As I
    warned, compressing the extremes toward 0 and π makes the cos flatness
    worse. A clean negative result: the angle mapping matters, and MinMax was
    the better choice.
  - The lower learning rate doesn't fix the early checkpoints. Its seeds still
    pick epochs 1–4 as best. So the early best epochs aren't caused by the step
    size. More likely the model reaches its best ranking fast, and the noisy
    validation then picks among near-equal checkpoints.
  - The combination is the most extreme: it has the best single result of all
    (0.739 on seed 0, above the logistic regression), but averages lower. It
    includes arctan, which drags it down. Full data alone looks like the better
    choice.
  - Weighting the loss towards the real fraud rate (share 0.25) is worse than 
    balanced (0.5). Balanced weighting suits AUPRC better here.
  - The baseline reproduces exactly: its rerun gives 0.421 on seed 0, the same
    as the earlier run. Good evidence that the pipeline is deterministic with
    fixed seeds, which the brief asks for.

  These are 4-seed differences of 0.01–0.05; only arctan's drop is clearly
  beyond the test noise. The angle runs should finish within about 5 minutes.
  Then I'll run the full report with validation AUPRC and the paired bootstrap
  against angle_scaled. The baseline needs about another hour; after that I'll
  build the final tables against it.

  All keep ρ ≥ 0.98 at p = 0.05 and the same 25-noise-location circuit.
  Full-data training costs 3% more training executions (565k vs 549k).

  What we learned
  1. Full training data gives a small, consistent gain that isn't statistically 
     established: +0.004–0.005, positive in 79–91% of bootstrap resamples. It
     agrees with the hard-negatives explanation, but the effect is tiny.
  2. Two clear negative results:
     - Arctan mapping: −0.07, and very unstable. Squeezing extremes toward 0 and
       π, where cos is flat, destroys the ordering. The angle mapping really
       matters.
     - Loss weighted toward the real fraud rate (0.25): −0.015, significant.
       Balanced weighting fits AUPRC better.
  3. A lower learning rate changes nothing. The early best epochs aren't caused
     by the step size.
  4. The gap to the logistic regression is no longer significant. For
     angle_scaled it's +0.020 with an interval that includes 0. Before scaling,
     for angle_pruned, it was +0.030 and significant. You can say "statistically
     indistinguishable from the classical reference on this test set". Don't
     say "as good as".

  An important methodological catch: validation picks the wrong model here. If
  you strictly choose the model with the best validation AUPRC, you'd pick combo
  (0.690), which is among the worst on test (0.655).
  - Why: the validation AUPRC reported is that of the best of 30 checkpoints,
    all scored on the same validation set. That makes it optimistically biased,
    and the bias is largest for unstable models: combo has the widest spread.
  - So "highest validation score" rewards volatility, not quality.
  - This is a real, presentable insight about model selection under rare events
    (only 99 validation frauds).

  How to handle it honestly:
  - Option A: use a stability-aware rule, mean validation AUPRC minus its
    standard deviation over seeds. It picks angle_scaled_full (0.664), ahead of
    angle_scaled (0.659), with combo far behind (0.644). But since we've already
    seen test, say on the slide that the rule was adopted after this
    observation.
  - Option B (cleanest): split validation in two, one half for choosing
    checkpoints and the other for choosing models. It needs reruns of the
    finalists, which is cheap for angle models.
  - Simplest defensible choice: stay with angle_scaled, chosen on validation
    before these variants existed, and present full as an ablation with a small,
    unproven gain.

  Recommended final lineup:
  - main model: angle_scaled or angle_scaled_full (the difference is within
    noise);
  - cheap training: angle_pruned_spsa;
  - negative results: arctan, loss share 0.25, output-only scaling, scaling +
    SPSA, light-cone pruning under this noise model.

  Still running: the baseline rerun (seed 1 of 5, its seed 0 reproduced the old
  run exactly), about another hour. Then I'll build the final tables, ledger,
  figures and bootstrap intervals against the required baseline. In the
  meantime, do you want option B (split validation) for the two or three
  finalists? It would take about 20–30 minutes alongside the baseline.

  How to read it
  1. The accurate Adam angle models cost about as much as the baseline: 93%
     under the Tracker count. Training is a bit cheaper (549–565k vs 726k), but
     validation is 76% of their cost, because the full validation set is scored
     after every epoch. Their gain is accuracy and robustness (0.68 vs 0.41 at p
     = 0.05), not cost.
  2. Full-data training costs only 3% more (565k vs 549k training executions).
     It uses the same number of batches per epoch, so its small accuracy gain is
     almost free.
  3. angle_pruned_spsa is the cost result: 3.8% of the baseline under the
     Tracker count, with 0.662 AUPRC at p = 0.05, against the baseline's 0.405.
     - With the one-off pilot spread over the 5 seeds, it's about 126k per run,
       about 5% of the baseline (about 20× cheaper).
     - All 5 seeds together: 5 × 92k + 172k ≈ 633k executions, against 12.2M for
       the baseline.
  4. Scaling doesn't pay off with SPSA: angle_scaled_spsa costs 8% more than
     angle_pruned_spsa and scores lower (0.642 vs 0.662).

  The obvious gap in the cost story: an accurate Adam model with cheap
  validation. Validation dominates, and the cheap-validation option is already
  in the code (val_subset=True). By formula:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────┬───────┬─────────────┐
  │     projected variant     │ training │ validation │ total │ vs Tracker  │
  │                           │          │            │       │  baseline   │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ angle_scaled_full, cheap  │ 565,440  │ 88,290     │ ~654k │ ~27%        │
  │ validation, 30 epochs     │          │            │       │             │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ the same, 10 epochs       │ 188,480  │ 29,430     │ ~218k │ ~9%         │
  └───────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────┴───────┴─────────────┘

  These are projections; they still need a run to check accuracy. Most best

✻ Churned for 2m 28s · done Saturday 21:03

❯ How did you simulate noise and how are the runs this fast? It shouldn't be    
  possible on a non quantum computer.                                           

● It is possible, because these circuits are tiny. Simulating a quantum computer
  on a classical one is exact and cheap for a few qubits. It only becomes
  impossible as the number of qubits grows, because the cost doubles (or, with
  noise, quadruples) with every qubit.

  How the noise is simulated

  The noise model is the official one from the notebook (noisy_qnode, Part 10),
  which main.py uses unchanged:
  1. The circuit is decomposed into the native gate set {RX, RY, RZ, Rot, H,
     CNOT, CZ}.
  2. After every gate, a depolarizing channel of probability p is inserted on
     every qubit the gate touches (qml.noise.insert(..., 
     qml.DepolarizingChannel, p, position="all")). That channel replaces the
     qubit's state with a random one with probability p.
  3. A pure state vector can't represent that randomness, so the circuit runs on
     PennyLane's default.mixed device. It tracks the full density matrix ρ, a
     2ⁿ × 2ⁿ matrix describing the probabilistic mixture of states.
  4. Each gate is a matrix multiplication U ρ U†. Each noise channel is a
     weighted sum of a few such products (its Kraus operators).
  5. At the end, ⟨Z⟩ = Tr(Z ρ) is computed exactly.

  Why it's fast
  - The numbers are small. A 4-qubit density matrix has 4⁴ = 256 entries; at 6
    qubits, 4,096. A full circuit is a few dozen small matrix products per
    transaction, which a laptop does in microseconds.
  - Transactions are batched. Thousands of transactions go through the circuit
    at once as one batched tensor operation, not one by one.
  - There are no shots. Hardware estimates ⟨Z⟩ by repeating each circuit about
    1,024 times and averaging the noisy measurements. The simulator computes the
    exact expectation directly, as if with infinitely many shots, in one pass.
  - Gradients are almost free. Training uses backpropagation through the
    simulator, about one extra pass. On hardware each gradient needs 1+2P
    separate circuit runs (parameter shift).

  That's why the cost ledger counts what a real quantum computer would need:
  executions and shots (e.g. 2.4 billion shots for the baseline). The simulator
  doesn't actually perform that work.

  Where it stops being possible
  - A state vector needs 2ⁿ numbers: about 16 GB at 30 qubits. A density matrix
    needs 4ⁿ: already about 16 GB at 15 qubits.
  - That's why the brief advises staying at 6 qubits or fewer for noisy runs.
    Going from 4 to 6 qubits made each pass about 4× slower here. At 20–30
    qubits, exact simulation is out of reach, which is where quantum hardware
    could matter.
  - At 4–6 qubits there's no quantum advantage possible at all. That's also why
    the logistic regression is a fair, and hard, reference.

  Caveats for your slides
  - Exact simulation flatters the results. With no shot noise, scores are
    perfectly precise. On hardware, 1,024 shots give a standard error of about
    0.03 on ⟨Z⟩, which would blur rankings of transactions whose scores are
    close. (This was point 18 of the ZNE suggestions.)
  - The noise model is idealised: only depolarizing noise after gates, no
    readout errors, no crosstalk, no coherent over-rotations, no decoherence
    while idle. It's the model the brief prescribes, so everyone is compared the
    same way, but real devices are messier. Your strong robustness result
    (almost no loss at p = 0.05) holds for this model specifically.

✻ Baked for 1m 10s · done Saturday 21:41

❯ How did you simulate noise and how are the runs this fast? It shouldn't be    
  possible on a non quantum computer.                                           

● You've asked this again, so I'll show it concretely instead of just restating
  it. Here is a small demonstration: a noisy circuit simulated by PennyLane,
  checked against a pen-and-paper formula, plus the actual memory sizes and
  timings.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● A classical computer can simulate a quantum computer exactly, including noise.
  It's just that the cost grows exponentially with the number of qubits. At 4–6
  qubits that cost is tiny. The demo above shows it with numbers:

  1. The noise is real maths, checked by hand. One qubit, rotated by RY(1.1) and
  then hit by a 5% depolarizing channel:
  - PennyLane gives ⟨Z⟩ = 0.423356.
  - The textbook formula for that channel (the state is replaced by a random one
    with probability p) gives (1 − 4p/3)·cos θ = 0.423356. Identical.

  2. What's stored is small. The simulator keeps a density matrix, a table of 4ⁿ
  complex numbers describing the noisy, probabilistic state.
  - At 4 qubits it's 16 × 16 = 256 numbers.
  - Its purity is 0.766, below 1, so the noise really did mix the state.
  - Each gate and each noise channel is one small matrix multiplication on that
    table.

  3. It's fast because the table is tiny, and stops being possible as it grows:

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │     qubits     │  density matrix  │        memory         │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 4 (your model) │ 256 numbers      │ 4 KB                  │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 6              │ 4,096            │ 66 KB                 │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 10             │ about 1 million  │ 17 MB                 │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 15             │ about 1 billion  │ 17 GB                 │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 20             │ about 1 trillion │ 17,600 GB, impossible │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  So "it shouldn't be possible" is right for large circuits, just not for ours.
  That's why the brief says to stay at 6 qubits or fewer for noisy runs.

  4. The simulator does far less work than hardware would. The real angle
  circuit (25 noise channels, p = 0.05) scores 10,000 transactions in 5.4 
  seconds, about 0.5 ms each.
  - The simulator computes ⟨Z⟩ exactly, in one pass per transaction.
  - A quantum computer can't read ⟨Z⟩ directly. It has to run each circuit about
    1,024 times and average the 0/1 outcomes: 10.2 million circuit runs for the
    same 10,000 transactions.
  - That's why the cost ledger counts hardware executions and shots. It
    estimates what a real device would need, not what the laptop did.

  In short: the noise is simulated exactly with the official model, by matrix
  algebra on a 16 × 16 matrix. The runs are fast because 4–6 qubits is far below
  the point (about 15 qubits for noisy simulation) where classical computers
  can no longer keep up. The flip side, worth one sentence on a slide: exact
  simulation has no shot noise, so it's slightly more optimistic than real
  hardware, where 1,024 shots leave about ±0.03 of uncertainty on each ⟨Z⟩.

  - The 6-qubit runs are much slower than my single-process timing suggested.

❯ I want to try a hybrid classic ML/quantum ML approach, as in encode the       
  original data with a classic model instead of the ZZ/angle encoders, then     
  continue normaly with the rest of the pipeline. What classic model options do 
  I have and what are the pros and cons of each?                                

● Yes, and the brief explicitly allows it ("classical layers may prepare the
  inputs"), on two conditions you have to protect:
  - the classification must still come from the circuit;
  - the logistic regression reference must use the same inputs. If the classical
    encoder becomes the real classifier, both conditions break, and a jury will
    notice.

  So every option below is judged on two questions: how much it helps, and how
  much classifying it does by itself.

  Unsupervised encoders (they don't see the labels; safest)

  option: PCA (current)
  pros: simple, fast, fitted on training only; the reference point
  cons: keeps the highest-variance directions, not the most fraud-discriminative

  ones
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  option: Autoencoder (PyTorch, e.g. 30 → 16 → 4)
  pros: nonlinear compression; can train on all 170k training rows, labels not
  needed; its reconstruction error is a natural fraud signal (frauds are
  anomalies)
  cons: needs tuning (architecture, epochs); no guarantee the bottleneck keeps
  what separates fraud; adds a training stage to describe and justify
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  option: ICA / whitened PCA
  pros: cheap variants of PCA that can expose non-Gaussian structure
  cons: rarely much better than PCA on this dataset (V1–V28 are already PCA
  components)

  Supervised selection or reduction (they see the labels; stronger, but watch 
  the leakage)

  option: Feature selection (mutual information or L1 logistic regression; picks

  e.g. V14, V17, V12, V10)
  pros: cheap, very interpretable ("these 4 features"), likely the biggest gain
  for the effort
  cons: the logistic regression gains just as much, so the quantum-vs-classical
  gap probably doesn't shrink
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  option: PLS (partial least squares)
  pros: gives n supervised linear components, like PCA but aimed at the label
  cons: the same "lifts the classical model too" issue; less standard, so it
  needs a sentence of explanation
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  option: LDA
  pros: the classic supervised projection
  cons: for 2 classes it gives only 1 component: too few for 4+ qubits

  Encoders trained jointly with the circuit (the real hybrid)

  option: Linear layer 30 → n trained together with the VQC (PyTorch, the
  encoder
  learns what angles the circuit needs)
  pros: the most "hybrid" option; a natural extension of your input scaling
  (which is the per-feature special case); small, hard to make the classifier
  on its own
  cons: gradients through the circuit's inputs cost extra on hardware: about +2n

  executions per example (Tracker counts it); can overfit with 295 frauds
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  option: Small neural network (MLP) → n angles, trained jointly
  pros: the most expressive
  cons: high risk: the MLP can do the classification and leave the circuit as a
  mere readout, which goes against the rules; overfits easily; the hardest to
  defend
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  option: Gradient-boosted trees as an encoder (e.g. tree outputs or leaf
  embeddings as angles)
  pros: very strong on tabular fraud data
  cons: the trees are the classifier, so the circuit adds nothing. Avoid.

  Ablations any hybrid needs to be credible:
  1. Encoder + logistic regression on the same n outputs, the brief's required
     classical reference.
  2. Encoder + a linear head with no circuit: shows the circuit adds something
     beyond the encoder.
  3. The same encoder at p = 0.01 and 0.05: check that robustness survives. With
     angle encoding the circuit stays small, so it should.

  Fit everything on training data only. A jointly trained encoder must also be
  included in the cost ledger. It costs no circuit executions to run, but its
  gradients need extra parameter shifts.

  My recommendation
  1. Feature selection by mutual information (n = 4): cheapest, interpretable,
     likely a large absolute gain. Present it honestly as a better input
     pipeline, since the logistic regression benefits too.
  2. Linear 30 → n layer trained jointly: the genuinely hybrid, defensible
     version. It answers whether a learned encoding beats PCA for the quantum
     model specifically.
  3. Autoencoder: if time allows, as the unsupervised, nonlinear option.

  I'd skip the MLP and the trees: their gains would be hard to credit to the
  quantum part.

  Both 1 and 2 plug into the current angle_scaled pipeline: the encoder replaces
  PCA, and the angle circuit stays the same. Should I implement those two? The
  5-qubit runs are still going, so I'd add them to the queue.

  damage for noise-aware training to undo. So the likely outcome is "no gain",
  which is still a valid, reportable result. A gain could come instead from a
  regularising effect: noise acts like smoothing during training.

  with gradients through the density matrix. On hardware the cost stays the
  same, since the noise is free there. I'll also add the angle version of the

● The noise-aware experiment is running: four models, 5 seeds each.

  What's new in main.py
  - Adam can now train inside the official noise model (noise_train option).
    Every batch runs on the noisy density-matrix simulator, and gradients flow
    through it. Options:
    - levels: the noise level per batch. One value means fixed noise; several
      values means one is picked at random for each batch (noise injection, as
      in QuantumNAT).
    - p_val: the noise level at which validation chooses the checkpoint.
  - Cost: the same as noiseless training. On hardware the noise is free, and the
    ledger counts it that way.

  Running now (output files task2_v6_<model>_*):

✻ Crunched for 1m 48s · done Saturday 21:57
❯ Would a KAN work as a good encoder for the hybrid approach?                   

● Yes, it's a well-motivated choice, more than a generic MLP, with one caveat to
  manage.

  What a KAN is here. A Kolmogorov–Arnold Network (Liu et al., 2024) puts a
  learnable one-variable function (a small spline) on every connection and sums
  them at each node. A one-layer KAN from your features to n angles computes,
  for each qubit i:

    angleᵢ = Σⱼ φᵢⱼ(xⱼ)

  That's a sum of learned, nonlinear per-feature curves.

  Why it fits your pipeline in particular
  1. It generalises what already worked. Your input scaling aᵢ·xᵢ + bᵢ is the
     special case where each φ is a straight line. A KAN lets that curve bend.
  2. It aims at your diagnosed weakness. The precision-recall curves showed the
     VQC losing at the top of the ranking, plausibly because cos flattens near 0
     and π. A learned per-feature warp can spread out exactly the regions the
     circuit compresses. The arctan result showed that a fixed warp hurts; a
     learned one can adapt instead.
  3. It's interpretable. You can plot each learned φ ("this is what the encoder
     does to V14"). That's a strong slide for the "understanding of the method"
     criterion, and it's something an MLP can't give you.
  4. Originality: KAN + VQC hybrids are recent. It isn't standard practice, so
     it should count as your own method, as long as you cite the KAN paper and
     don't overclaim.

  Risks
  1. It could become the classifier. A 30-input KAN can learn something close to
     a fraud score on its own (a one-layer KAN with one output is essentially a
     generalised additive model, a classical classifier). Then the circuit
     would just pass it through, which goes against "the classification must
     come from the circuit". You'd need two ablations: KAN + logistic regression
     (the brief's reference, on the KAN's outputs) and KAN + linear head 
     without the circuit. If those match the hybrid, the circuit isn't
     contributing.
  2. Overfitting. There are only 295 training frauds. Each connection has
     roughly 5–8 spline coefficients, so a 30 → 4 KAN already has about 1,000
     parameters, against 15 in the circuit. You'd need a small grid,
     regularisation, or the full training set (the class-weighted sampler helps
     here).
  3. Outliers. Splines live on a fixed grid. Outside it they extend almost
     linearly, which is the same extreme-value issue as before: frauds are often
     outliers. Standardise the inputs and use a grid covering the training
     range, plus the residual linear term KANs have by default.
  4. Hardware cost. Training the encoder jointly needs gradients with respect to
     the circuit's inputs, which costs about 2n more executions per example (4
     qubits: 31 → about 39). That goes in the ledger. Running the trained KAN
     costs nothing.
  5. Tooling. pykan is PyTorch-based, which the rules allow, but it's heavy. A
     minimal KAN layer (Gaussian basis functions, about 30 lines of PyTorch,
     "FastKAN"-style) is easier to control and to explain.

  How I'd do it, in order of risk:
  1. Per-feature KAN on the n PCA components (each angle depends on one feature
     only, angleᵢ = φᵢ(xᵢ)). This is the minimal-risk version: a direct
     nonlinear upgrade of input scaling, too weak to classify on its own, about
     40 parameters. Start here.
  2. Full KAN from the 30 raw features to n, with regularisation and the two
     ablations. More power, more risk; worth it if (1) helps.
  3. In both cases, compare against a plain linear 30 → n encoder trained the
     same way, so the gain can be attributed to the KAN's nonlinearity rather
     than just to having a learned encoder.

  So yes: as a per-feature warp it's a natural, defensible next step, and the
  full version is a good research experiment as long as the ablations come with
  it.

  The noise-aware and 5-/6-qubit runs are still going. Should I implement the
  per-feature KAN (and the linear-encoder comparison) to run after them?

    compensating for it during training.

✻ Sautéed for 10s · done Saturday 23:11

❯ Go ahead with the per-feature KAN encoder                                     

● The per-feature KAN fits into the current setup as a direct upgrade of input
  scaling. For each qubit i, the angle becomes

    angleᵢ = aᵢ·xᵢ + bᵢ + Σₖ cᵢₖ · exp(−((xᵢ − μₖ)/h)²)

  that is, the linear part plus a learned curve built from 6 Gaussian bumps
  spread over [0, π]. That's the Gaussian-basis ("FastKAN") form of a KAN layer
  restricted to one input per output. The coefficients start at 0, so training
  starts exactly from angle_scaled. That makes angle_scaled the clean ablation:
  same everything, but the warp can only be a straight line.

      317 +    centers = torch.linspace(0, np.pi, k, dtype=torch.float64)
      318 +    width = np.pi / (k - 1)                                   
      319 +    return torch.exp(-((torch.as_tensor(x, dtype=torch.float64
          +)[..., None] - centers) / width) ** 2)                        
      320 +                                                              
      321 +                                                              
      322 +def kan_coefficients(weights, cfg):                           
      323 +    """Coeficientii KAN (n_qubits x kan_basis), stocati in ran
          +durile de dupa input_scaling."""                              
      324 +    if not cfg.get("kan_basis"):                              
      325 +        return None                                           
     … 21 more lines (ctrl+o to expand)

  Conclusion: training inside the noise model makes no measurable difference for
  the angle circuits. Every interval includes zero, including training at
  exactly the noise level being evaluated (nat05 at p = 0.05).
  - The reason is clear: the noiseless angle models already lose only 0.002
    AUPRC between p = 0 and p = 0.05, so there's nothing for noise-aware
    training to recover.
  - The cost is the same, since noise is free on hardware, but simulation time
    is several times higher.

  For the slides, this completes a consistent story across all the noise-related
  experiments:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │            approach            │      ZZ circuits       │    angle     │
  │                                │                        │   circuits   │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ fewer gates in the light cone  │                        │ almost       │
  │ (smaller circuit, angle        │ large gain at p = 0.05 │ immune to    │
  │ encoding)                      │                        │ noise        │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │                                │ ranking (ρ) more       │              │
  │ noise-aware training           │ stable, AUPRC          │ no effect    │
  │                                │ unchanged              │              │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │                                │ no effect (removed     │              │
  │ light-cone pruning             │ noise can't reach the  │ –            │
  │                                │ readout)               │              │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ ZNE (implemented, not run in   │                        │ little       │
  │ full)                          │ –                      │ headroom     │
  │                                │                        │ left         │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  Reducing noise at its source beats compensating for it. That's an answer to
  the brief's question about when noise-aware training helps: it doesn't, once
  the circuit is small enough to be robust.

● The image is the learned-curves figure I just generated
  (task2_v7_angle_kan_full_kan_curves.png). Here's how to read it, together with
  the KAN results.

  KAN results (test AUPRC, 5 seeds, paired bootstrap against the identical model
  without the KAN curves):

  p = 0.05: 0.698 ± 0.044

  val AUPRC at p = 0.05: 0.699 ± 0.009
  F1 at p = 0.05: 0.727
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  p = 0.01: 0.690 ± 0.019
  p = 0.05: 0.686 ± 0.022

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  model: angle_kan
  p = 0.01: 0.679 ± 0.017
  p = 0.05: 0.679 ± 0.021
  Δ at p = 0.05 vs ablation: −0.005 [−0.062, +0.061] vs angle_scaled
  val AUPRC at p = 0.05: 0.676 ± 0.010
  F1 at p = 0.05: 0.729
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  model: angle_scaled
  p = 0.01: 0.685 ± 0.022
  p = 0.05: 0.682 ± 0.016
  Δ at p = 0.05 vs ablation: –
  val AUPRC at p = 0.05: 0.675 ± 0.016
  F1 at p = 0.05: 0.789

  Per seed at p = 0.05, angle_kan_full scores 0.710, 0.662, 0.744, 0.642, 0.730.
  Two seeds are above the logistic regression.

  What it means
  1. The KAN only helps with the full training data. On the 590 balanced rows it
     adds nothing: best epoch 2, and the 24 extra parameters overfit
     immediately. With the full training set it keeps learning (best epoch 17 on
     average) and reaches the highest mean AUPRC of any VQC: 0.698, only 0.005 
     below the logistic regression.
  2. It's the first model that validation picks clearly and stably. Validation
     AUPRC is 0.699 with a small spread (± 0.009). Unlike combo, it also wins
     under the stability-aware rule (mean − std: 0.690 vs 0.664). So choosing it
     doesn't depend on peeking at test, and the test result agrees.
  3. But the gain isn't established, and it's less stable. The test spread
     doubles (± 0.044), and the interval against its ablation is wide (−0.047 to
     +0.069). Honest wording: "the best mean, not significantly better than
     without the KAN".
  4. F1 at the validation threshold drops, from 0.79 to 0.73, while AUPRC stays
     equal or improves. The KAN's sharp warps make the score distribution differ
     more between validation and test, so the threshold chosen on validation
     transfers worse. Since threshold metrics are part of the judging, this is a
     real trade-off to show.

  What the curves show (top row: angle into RY against the PCA angle, one line
  per seed, dashed = plain RY(x); bottom row: training data):
  - The encoding wastes most of the angle range. The legitimate transactions
    pile into one very narrow bin per component (PC1 ≈ 3.1, PC2 ≈ 2.45, PC3 ≈
    1.5, PC4 ≈ 1.25). The frauds spread across the rest. MinMax scaling is
    stretched by the fraud outliers, so the class boundary sits in a sliver of
    about 0.1 rad.
  - The KAN learns to zoom in on those slivers. Across seeds, the curves are
    steepest where frauds and legitimate transactions meet: PC2 rises steeply
    between about 2.3 and 3.0, PC3 between about 1.0 and 2.0, PC1 near the
    legitimate peak at about 3.0. It spreads the decision region over more of
    the rotation, which is exactly what the circuit needs.
  - The curves differ a lot between seeds. The circuit's own weights can
    compensate for different warps (a rotation added before an RY can be
    absorbed later), so individual curves aren't unique and shouldn't be read
    one by one. The consistent part is where they're steep. That's also a likely
    reason for the larger seed spread.

  The idea this points to. If the main benefit is spreading the dense legitimate
  cluster, a fixed transform might capture most of it without the KAN's
  instability: a quantile transform fitted on the training data, which maps each
  component to a uniform distribution. It's the opposite of arctan, which
  squeezed the extremes and failed. It's cheap (one preprocessing option and a
  fast angle run), deterministic, and the logistic regression gets the same
  inputs. If it matches the KAN, that's a strong, simple story: the KAN told us
  what the encoding was missing.

  For the presentation: angle_kan_full is a good "own method" candidate (hybrid
  KAN + VQC, picked on validation, best mean AUPRC, with interpretable curves),
  as long as you present its higher variance and lower F1 next to it.

  Should I add the quantile-transform variant as the next fast run?

✻ Brewed for 2m 2s · done Saturday 23:23

❯ Ok, but can you first explain the quantile transform?                         

● A quantile transform replaces each value by its rank within the training data,
  expressed as a fraction, and then rescales it to an angle.

  How it works, step by step (for each principal component separately)
  1. Fit, on training data only: sort the training values of the component and
     record their quantiles: the value below which 1% of the data lies, 2%, and
     so on.
  2. Transform any value x: find what fraction of the training data lies below
     x. That's its percentile, between 0 and 1 (sklearn's QuantileTransformer
     interpolates linearly between the stored quantiles).
  3. Map to an angle: angle = π × percentile.

  So the training data ends up uniformly spread over [0, π]. Every 10% of the
  data gets the same 0.31 rad of rotation, however tightly packed those points
  were originally.

  Compared with what we've tried

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │  transform  │ where angle range goes  │          effect here           │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ spread evenly over the  │ fraud outliers stretch the     │
  │ MinMax      │ value range, from       │ range, so the dense legitimate │
  │ (current)   │ smallest to largest     │  cluster gets a sliver (about  │
  │             │ training value          │ 0.1 rad on PC2)                │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ arctan      │ most range near the     │ squeezed the frauds' region    │
  │ (tried,     │ centre; extremes        │ and lost their ordering        │
  │ −0.07)      │ squeezed toward 0 and π │                                │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ spread evenly over the  │ the legitimate cluster, where  │
  │ quantile    │ data: dense regions get │ the class boundary sits, gets  │
  │             │  more angle, sparse     │ far more angle                 │
  │             │ regions less            │                                │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  On PC2, for example: in the balanced training set about half the rows are
  legitimate and sit in that narrow peak. Under a quantile transform they'd
  occupy about half of [0, π] (≈ 1.5 rad instead of ≈ 0.1 rad), roughly 15× more
  resolution exactly where the KAN learned to zoom in.

  Useful properties
  - Monotone: it never changes the order of values within a feature, it only
    changes the spacing. No information is thrown away, apart from ties.
  - Robust to outliers: an extreme fraud just maps to a percentile near 100%. It
    can't stretch the scale for everyone else, as it does with MinMax.
  - Fixed and deterministic: no extra parameters to train, so no extra seed
    variance. That's the KAN's weakness.
  - It doesn't use the labels directly, so it can't act as a hidden classifier.

  Caveats
  - Which data you fit it on matters:
    - on the 590 balanced rows (as the PCA is now): half the angle range goes to
      frauds, half to legitimate transactions. Balanced resolution for both
      classes.
    - on the full training set (99.8% legitimate): nearly the whole range goes
      to spreading out legitimate transactions, and all frauds get crammed into
      the top few percent, losing their internal ordering. Probably worse for
      detection, though it would give the finest resolution among legitimate
      transactions.

    I'd fit on the balanced rows, and maybe test the full-data fit as an
    ablation.
  - Values outside the training range: test transactions beyond the most extreme
    training value all map to exactly 0 or π. That's the same clipping as
    MinMax, and it was rare here (about 2% of frauds).
  - The flatness of cos at 0 and π remains, so the most extreme percentiles are
    still compressed by the circuit itself. Fitting to a slightly narrower range
    such as [0.05π, 0.95π] would soften that; the trainable input scaling can
    also adjust it.
  - The logistic regression changes too, since it gets the same transformed
    inputs. That keeps the comparison fair, but the classical reference might
    improve as well.

  How it would be tested: angle_scaled_full with the quantile transform instead
  of MinMax, fitted on the balanced rows, compared with angle_scaled_full
  (MinMax) and angle_kan_full (learned warp). If quantile matches the KAN, the
  conclusion is that the KAN discovered that the encoding needs to spread the
  dense region, and a fixed transform does that with less variance and better
  threshold stability.

  Should I run it?
