  │                              │ +0.031       │ likely gain, not         │
  │ quantile alone − MinMax      │ [−0.017,     │ established              │
  │                              │ +0.085]      │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ KAN + quantile − quantile    │ +0.017       │ the KAN still adds a bit │
  │ alone                        │ [−0.009,     │  on top                  │
  │                              │ +0.045]      │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ KAN + quantile − logistic    │ +0.006       │ on par with the          │
  │ regression (same inputs)     │ [−0.039,     │ classical reference      │
  │                              │ +0.056]      │                          │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  What this means
  1. The diagnosis was right. MinMax left the dense legitimate cluster only a
     few hundredths of a radian. Spreading it out with the quantile transform
     lifts the VQC by about 0.03, and the logistic regression too (0.703 →
     0.730). So it's a genuine input fix, not something quantum-specific.
  2. The KAN keeps adding on top of it. With quantile inputs it's both better
     and more stable than with MinMax (± 0.028 vs ± 0.044), and its F1 problem
     largely goes away (0.765 vs 0.727; still a bit below the 0.79 of the MinMax
     models).
  3. The VQC now equals the classical reference on the same inputs: 0.737 vs
     0.730 at p = 0.05, winning on 3 of 5 seeds. Phrase it carefully:
     "statistically indistinguishable from the logistic regression", not
     "better". Before this, the classical model was always ahead.
  4. Validation picks this model too. angle_kan_full_quant has the highest mean
     validation AUPRC at both headline noise levels (0.717 and 0.713), so
     choosing it doesn't depend on test.
  5. The fit choice matters. Fitting the quantile transform on the full training
     set crams all frauds into the top few percent and hurts both models (the
     logistic regression even falls to 0.593). That's a clean ablation for why
     the balanced fit was chosen.
  6. Robustness and cost are unchanged. AUPRC doesn't drop from p = 0 to p =
     0.05 (0.731 → 0.737). ρ is 0.978, slightly lower than before but still
     high. The circuit is the same (20 gates, 25 noise locations) and training
     costs the same. The models also train longer now (best epochs 16–21 instead
     of 2–7): with the inputs spread out, there's more for the gradients to
     work with.

  The improvement ladder for your slides (test AUPRC at p = 0.05):

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────┐
  │           step           │          model          │ AUPRC │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ baseline (ZZ, 93 gates)  │ baseline                │ 0.405 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ + smaller ZZ circuit     │ pruned                  │ 0.541 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ + angle encoding         │ angle_pruned            │ 0.669 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ + input / output scaling │ angle_scaled            │ 0.682 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ + full training data     │ angle_scaled_full       │ 0.686 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ + quantile transform     │ angle_scaled_full_quant │ 0.720 │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ + per-feature KAN        │ angle_kan_full_quant    │ 0.737 │
  └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────┘

  Read it honestly: the first two steps are each clearly significant, while the
  later ones are individually within the test noise. Cumulatively they're a
  large gain, and together they close the gap to the classical reference.

  Still to do for the submission:
  - an SPSA version of the final model, for the cost story: angle_pruned_spsa
    with quantile inputs, cheap and likely to benefit too;
  - a final report collecting the baseline, the ladder, the classical
    references, the ledger and all figures in one place.

  Shall I run the SPSA-with-quantile model and then produce that final report?

  Paired bootstrap at p = 0.05 (95% intervals):
  - SPSA quantile − SPSA MinMax: −0.001 (Δ +0.009 the other way, [−0.056,
    +0.073]). No effect.
  - best Adam model − SPSA quantile: +0.080 [+0.026, +0.129], a significant gap.
  - logistic regression (same inputs) − SPSA quantile: +0.075 [+0.016, +0.136],
    also significant.
  - SPSA quantile − baseline: +0.246 [+0.189, +0.310]. The cheap model still
    beats the baseline by a wide margin.

  Why SPSA doesn't benefit
  - Adam needed more training to use the better inputs: its best epochs moved
    from 2–7 to 16–21 with quantile inputs. SPSA's budget is fixed at 600 noisy
    steps, and its best checkpoints came early (steps 100–400), so it doesn't
    get far enough to exploit the extra resolution.
  - The pilot was noisy. It picked a large step (0.1), but neighbouring settings
    scored very differently (0.680 vs 0.595 for c = 0.05 vs 0.15). With only
    150 steps and a noisy validation subset, the pilot can't reliably find good
    settings. That's the hyperparameter sensitivity Kübler et al. warned about,
    now measured.
  - Its F1 also drops (0.708 vs 0.763 with MinMax), so for SPSA the quantile
    inputs are, if anything, a step back.

  What this means for the cost story
  - The cheap model is still angle_pruned_spsa with MinMax inputs: 0.662 at 3.8%
    of the baseline cost (Tracker count), with better F1 and slightly less
    variance than the quantile version.
  - The honest trade-off now: about 25× cheaper training costs about 0.075 AUPRC
    against the best model (0.662 vs 0.737). Earlier, the cheap and expensive
    models were within 0.01, but the best Adam model has since moved up while
    SPSA hasn't.

  A better middle point is likely available. The best Adam model spends 75% of
  its cost on validation: full validation every epoch. With the cheap-validation
  option (val_subset=True, already implemented) it would cost about 654k
  executions, about 27% of the baseline, probably with similar accuracy, since
  validation only picks the checkpoint. That would give a much better
  cost/accuracy point than SPSA:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │  point on the cost curve  │  AUPRC at p =    │    cost vs baseline     │
  │                           │       0.05       │        (Tracker)        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ baseline                  │ 0.405            │ 100%                    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ best model, full          │ 0.737            │ 93%                     │
  │ validation                │                  │                         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ best model, cheap         │ ? (expected      │ ~27% (projected)        │
  │ validation                │ ~0.73)           │                         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ SPSA                      │ 0.662            │ 3.8%                    │
  └───────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  Should I run angle_kan_full_quant with cheap validation (30 epochs) to fill
  that gap? It's about 15 minutes. After that, I'd produce the final report with
  everything for the submission.

❯ go ahead with the cheap validation run                                        

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The cheap-validation run (angle_kan_full_quant_cheapval) has started: 5 seeds,
  the whole CPU, about 10–15 minutes. Results go to
  task2_v10_angle_kan_full_quant_cheapval_*.

  It's identical to the best model (KAN + quantile + full data, 30 epochs of
  Adam). The only change: after each epoch, the checkpoint is chosen on the
  stratified validation subset (all 99 frauds plus 5% of legitimate
  transactions, weighted AUPRC) instead of the full 56,962 rows. That cuts
  validation from 1,708,860 to about 88,000 executions per run, so the total
  drops from about 2.27M to about 654k, about 27% of the baseline under the
  Tracker count.

  When it finishes, the questions are:
  - Accuracy: does it stay close to 0.737 at p = 0.05? I'll compare it with the
    full-validation version using paired bootstrap. Validation only picks the
    checkpoint, so a small or zero loss is expected, though the noisier subset
    may sometimes pick a worse epoch.
  - Cost curve: where it sits between the SPSA model (3.8%, 0.662) and the
    full-validation version (93%, 0.737).

  After that, all the pieces for the submission are in place, and I'd produce
  the final report:
  - the improvement ladder;
  - baseline, final models and classical references at every noise level;
  - the cost ledger;
  - the precision-recall, noise, cost, seed and KAN-curve figures;
  - the bootstrap tables.

  │                            │ 0.04 │ 0.17 │ 7    │           │          │
  │                            │ 9    │ 7    │      │           │          │
  └────────────────────────────┴──────┴──────┴──────┴───────────┴──────────┘

  Paired bootstrap, cheap minus full validation: −0.028 [−0.047, −0.009] at p =
  0.01 and −0.020 [−0.037, −0.001] at p = 0.05. A small loss, but real: the
  intervals exclude zero.

  Why it loses a little. Training is identical; only the checkpoint choice
  differs. The subset has the same 99 frauds but only 5% of the legitimate
  transactions, so its AUPRC is noisier and sometimes picks a worse epoch.
  - Seed 1: epoch 12 instead of 16 → 0.673 instead of 0.730.
  - Seed 3: the noise happened to favour it → 0.747 instead of 0.706.
  - The spread across seeds grows a bit as a result (± 0.037 vs ± 0.028).

  The cost curve now has three solid points (test AUPRC at p = 0.05 against the
  training + validation cost, Tracker count):

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────┬────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │          point          │ AUPRC │    cost vs     │   AUPRC gain over    │
  │                         │       │    baseline    │       baseline       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ baseline                │ 0.405 │ 100%           │ –                    │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ best model, full        │ 0.737 │ 93%            │ +0.33                │
  │ validation              │       │                │                      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ best model, cheap       │ 0.717 │ 27%            │ +0.31                │
  │ validation              │       │                │                      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ SPSA                    │ 0.662 │ 3.8%           │ +0.26                │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────┴────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  - Cheap validation is the best trade-off: 95% of the best model's gain over
    the baseline for under a third of the cost. It still beats SPSA by +0.055
    and the baseline by about +0.31.
  - Each step down the cost curve costs accuracy: about −0.02 for cheap
    validation, then a further −0.055 for SPSA. Present it as a curve, so each
    team or budget can pick its point.
  - Against the classical reference: the full-validation model matches the
    logistic regression on the same inputs (0.737 vs 0.730). The
    cheap-validation one is slightly below (0.717), within its spread.

  One possible refinement, if you want to narrow the 0.02 gap: a larger
  validation subset (20% of legitimate transactions instead of 5%) would cost
  about 37% of the baseline instead of 27%, with a less noisy checkpoint choice.
  Optional; the current point is already a good result.

  That completes the experiments needed for the submission. Should I produce the
  final report next? It would include:
  - the improvement ladder;
  - the baseline, the final models (best, cheap-validation, SPSA) and their
    classical references at all four noise levels, with all protocol metrics;
  - the cost ledger, both formula and Tracker counts;
  - the bootstrap tables;
  - the figures: metrics vs noise, cost vs AUPRC, per-seed spread,
    precision-recall curves and KAN curves.

● The final report is generated. Everything is in the project folder as
  task2_final_*, built from the saved runs of the seven final models.

  Files

  F1, MCC, validation AUPRC) for every model and classical reference at p = 0,
  0.001, 0.01, 0.05, as mean ± std over 5 seeds

  Key statistical results (paired bootstrap, 400 test resamples):
  - Every final model beats the baseline at both headline noise levels, with
    intervals clear of zero. At p = 0.05: best model +0.326 [+0.273, +0.384],
    cheap validation +0.307, SPSA +0.254. The only exception is the smaller ZZ
    circuit at p = 0.01, which equals the baseline.
  - The best model matches the classical reference on the same inputs: +0.006
    [−0.039, +0.056] at p = 0.05. Statistically indistinguishable.
  - Cheap validation costs a small, real amount: −0.019 [−0.037, −0.003].
  - The baseline rerun reproduced the original run exactly (difference 0.0),
    confirming the pipeline is deterministic with fixed seeds.

  What the figures show
  - Noise: the baseline collapses at p = 0.05 (AUPRC 0.405, ρ 0.686). Every
    angle model stays flat across all noise levels.
  - Cost: the three final models form a clear cost/accuracy curve. Best accuracy
    at 93% of the baseline cost, 95% of the gain at 27%, 78% of the gain at
    3.8%.

  One small cosmetic issue: in the p = 0.05 panel of the cost figure, the
  cheap-validation label overlaps the "logistic regression (quantile)" text.

  Reproducing it. python main.py --models final --out task2_final runs the final
  set end to end (FINAL_MODELS in main.py). It reuses saved weights where
  available and otherwise retrains deterministically. python main.py --report 
  <prefixes> rebuilds the tables and figures from existing runs.

  For the presentation, the remaining material is in the earlier report files
  (task2_v2–task2_v10):
  - Negative results: light-cone pruning has no effect under noise, noise-aware
    training gives no gain, arctan mapping hurts, 5–6 qubits are worse,
    output-only scaling hurts, scaling hurts SPSA, and quantile inputs don't
    help SPSA.
  - The model-selection catch: the most volatile model can get the best
    validation score, which is why we looked at stability across seeds.
  - The improvement ladder: 0.405 → 0.541 → 0.669 → 0.682 → 0.686 → 0.720 →
    0.737 at p = 0.05.

  I can also turn this into a shareable report page if that would help with
  preparing the slides.

● The top level now holds only the final results and graphs, plus the project's
  own files. I moved the intermediate outputs into archive/ rather than deleting
  them, and deleted only __pycache__ (Python recreates it automatically).

  Top level now:
  - Final results (10 files): task2_final_results.csv, _per_seed.csv, _cost.csv,
    _bootstrap.csv, _vs_classical_bootstrap.csv, and the five figures
    _noise.png, _cost.png, _seeds.png, _pr.png, _kan_curves.png.
  - Project files: main.py, the Task 1 notebook, the brief PDF, data/ and
    split_indices.npz.
  - Files I didn't create, left as they were: the four optimized_cone_vqc*.py
    scripts and task2_validation_runs.csv. They were made between 01:26 and
    02:07, after my last report. If they're yours and you're done with them,
    they can go too; I didn't want to delete someone else's work.

  archive/ (225 MB):
  - archive/runs/: the 384 intermediate files from all experiments. These
    include the negative results and ablations for the slides, and the saved
    test scores.
  - archive/weights/: the 152 trained weight files.

  Two consequences of the move:
  - --report looks for its input files next to main.py, so rebuilding the final
    tables or figures (or bootstrapping new comparisons) means moving the needed
    task2_v* files back first.
  - python main.py --models final no longer finds saved weights, so it would
    retrain the final models from scratch. The results are the same, since
    training is deterministic, but it takes longer: about 1–1.5 hours, mostly
    the baseline.

  If you're sure you won't need any of it, deleting the archive with rm -rf 
  archive frees the 225 MB. I'd keep it at least until the slides are finished.

  - The five optimized_cone_vqc*.py scripts → archive/scripts/.
  - The four CSVs written by optimized_cone_vqc_ap.py → 
    archive/runs/optimized_cone_vqc_ap/. They have their own subfolder because
    archive/runs/ already held older files with the same names (task2_runs.csv,
    task2_summary.csv, task2_cost.csv from the first full main.py run). The move
    refuses to overwrite anything, so both versions are kept.

  Nothing was deleted this time.

  One thing to watch: optimized_cone_vqc_ap.py writes the same default file
  names as main.py (task2_runs.csv and so on). If both are run in this folder,
  they'll overwrite each other's results. Giving one of them a different output
  name (e.g. --out for main.py) avoids that. If you run the script again from
  archive/scripts/, note that it may look for data/ and split_indices.npz
  relative to its own location.

● I'll add SPSA as an alternative optimiser in fit_one and keep the rest of
  Peter's pipeline unchanged (encoders, circuits, remember() checkpointing,
  evaluation). It carries over what we measured in main.py:
  - SPSA with Spall's slowly decaying gains, the step size a calibrated from a
    target step;
  - the same minibatch for both evaluations, q averaged estimates per step;
  - stratified, weighted minibatches drawn from the full training set;
  - validation every N steps on his existing validation subset, with early
    stopping;
  - the executions it actually spends, recorded so that estimate_cost reports
    them instead of the parameter-shift formula.

  Comments stay in Romanian, like the rest of his file.

      357 +# ------------------------------------------------------------
          +---                                                           
      358 +# SPSA (Spall, 1992): gradient din 2 evaluari ale loss-ului, o
          +ricati parametri                                              
      359 +# ------------------------------------------------------------
          +---                                                           
      360 +                                                              
      361 +class StratifiedSampler:                                      
      362 +    """Minibatch-uri cu o proportie fixa de fraude, din TOT tr
          +ain-ul (dezechilibrat).                                       
      363 +                                                              
      364 +    Ponderea share_c / n_c pe fiecare exemplu din clasa c face
          + loss-ul pe batch un                                          
      365 +    estimator NEDEPLASAT al loss-ului echilibrat 0.5 * media(l
          +egit) + 0.5 * media(fraude),                                  
      366 +    adica exact ce estimeaza antrenarea pe setul subesantionat
          +."""                                                          
      367 +    def __init__(self, y, batch_size, pos_fraction, seed, pos_
          +share=.5):                                                    
      368 +        self.pools = [np.flatnonzero(y == 0), np.flatnonzero(y
          + == 1)]                                                       
      369 +        n_pos = int(np.clip(round(pos_fraction * batch_size), 
          +1, batch_size - 1))                                           
      370 +        self.counts = [batch_size - n_pos, n_pos]             
      371 +        shares = [1 - pos_share, pos_share]                   
      372 +        self.weights = torch.as_tensor(np.concatenate(        
      373 +            [np.full(n, s / n) for n, s in zip(self.counts, sh
          +ares)]), dtype=DT)                                            
      374 +        self.rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)                
      375 +                                                              
      376 +    def draw(self):                                           
      377 +        ix = np.concatenate([self.rng.choice(pool, size=n, rep
          +lace=False)                                                   
      378 +                             for pool, n in zip(self.pools, se
          +lf.counts)])                                                  
      379 +        return torch.as_tensor(ix), self.weights              
      380 +                                                              
      381 +

          +ului pe ACELASI minibatch,                                    
      384 +    cu perturbare Bernoulli +-1 pe toti parametrii deodata."""
      385 +    g = torch.zeros_like(w)                                   
      386 +    with torch.no_grad():

● SPSA is now in peter.py, and it works end to end: training, his checkpointing,
  evaluation and the cost file. His original code paths are unchanged (Adam
  remains the default), and his sanity checks all pass. A copy of the original
  is in my scratchpad if you want to compare:
  /tmp/claude-1000/-home-razvan-BreaQ-Quantum-AIM/7e546c33-2f59-419c-87e6-ef0ffc
  c1c825/scratchpad/peter_original.py.

  What I added (comments in Romanian, matching his file)
  - --optimizer spsa switches the clean training stage of fit_one from Adam to
    SPSA:
    - 2 loss evaluations per estimate, on the same minibatch, with ±1
      perturbations of all parameters at once;
    - --spsa-q (default 2) averaged estimates per step;
    - Spall's gains: a_k = a/(k+1+A)^0.602 and c_k = c/(k+1)^0.101, with A = 10%
      of the steps;
    - a calibrated so the first step moves each parameter by about
      --spsa-target-step radians.
  - --spsa-data full (the default) trains on the whole training set with
    stratified, weighted minibatches (StratifiedSampler). The loss stays an
    unbiased estimate of the balanced loss his undersampled training uses.
    --spsa-data balanced keeps his 590-row set instead.
  - Validation and early stopping: every --spsa-val-every steps (default 50), on
    his existing validation subset (--val-negatives), through his remember().
    It stops after --spsa-patience validations without improvement (default 6).
    The only change to remember() is that it now returns whether it improved.
  - Cost: the checkpoint records the executions SPSA actually used, and
    estimate_cost reports those instead of the parameter-shift formula. cost.csv
    gets a new optimizer column.
  - sanity gains an SPSA check: the average of 400 estimates must point the same
    way as the exact gradient (cosine 0.965).
  - quick mode caps SPSA at 100 steps.
  - The optional --joint-epochs stage still uses Adam with backprop after SPSA,
    and its cost is counted with his existing formula.

  What the test runs showed (seed 0, on his weighted validation subset, so not
  official numbers):

  ┌───────────────┬───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │    circuit    │      SPSA result      │              note               │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ AP 0.82 after 100     │ about the level of his          │
  │ reverse2      │ steps (0.824          │ logistic-regression diagnostic  │
  │               │ noiseless, 0.824 at p │ (0.81)                          │
  │               │  = 0.05)              │                                 │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ improved,     │ AP 0.818 by step 150, │ ~58k training executions vs     │
  │ full 600-step │  early stop at step   │ ~347k for his Adam default (12  │

  │ steps)        │                       │ flip                            │

  Advice for your teammate:
  - With the defaults, improved reaches its plateau by about step 150 in a full
    run.
  - Don't judge SPSA by quick mode: 100 steps can leave a model that started
    inverted not yet flipped. --spsa-target-step 0.1 or --init small (which his
    docstring already recommends) make it converge within the first 50–100
    steps.
  - As we found in main.py, SPSA is sensitive to its settings. If time allows,
    pick --spsa-target-step and --spsa-c with a short run on validation.

  Example: python peter.py train --tag spsa1 --models improved reverse2 
  --optimizer spsa --all-seeds, then python peter.py eval --tag spsa1 --models 
  improved reverse2 --noise 0 0.001 0.01 0.05 --official --all-seeds.

  vqc_runs/ didn't exist before, it's gone too. There's also a new file,
  quantic_mlp_budget.py, that appeared in the meantime. It isn't mine, so I left
  it alone.

  What it shows
  1. SPSA matches Adam on improved. 0.716 vs 0.713 at p = 0.05, within ±0.003 on
     4 of 5 seeds (paired t-test p = 0.84). It's even more stable across seeds
     (± 0.009 vs ± 0.030).
  2. On reverse2, SPSA is a bit behind: −0.013 at p = 0.05 and −0.019 at p =
     0.01. Seeds 3 and 4 account for it (−0.03); seeds 0–2 are equal. It's not
     significant with 5 seeds (p = 0.17–0.40).
  3. All four models are within about 0.01 of Peter's logistic regression on the
     same MLP angles (0.720). Adam reverse2 is slightly above (+0.005), SPSA
     improved slightly below (−0.004). Statistically, all on par with the
     classical reference.
  4. SPSA saves less here than in main.py, because Peter's Adam setup is already
     cheap: 12 epochs on 590 rows, validating every 3 epochs on a 5,099-row
     subset. So the savings are 3.7× for improved and 2.5× for reverse2, not
     ~25×.
     - Training alone drops by 4–6× (347k → 56k and 248k → 61k).
     - SPSA validates more often than Adam (every 50 steps, 8–9 checks, vs 4 for
       Adam). Its validation cost is higher (44–48k vs 20k), now almost half
       its total. --spsa-val-every 100 would roughly halve that.
  5. One collapsed ranking: improved / SPSA seed 1 has ρ = 0.136 at p = 0.05,
     while its AUPRC is fine (0.713). It's the effect we saw in main.py: noise
     reshuffles the many legitimate transactions with nearly equal scores, while
     the frauds stay on top. ρ is the jury's robustness metric, so this seed is
     worth mentioning. Adam reverse2 also has a weaker ρ seed (0.774), while
     SPSA reverse2 is the most stable (0.976–0.996).

  Bottom line for Peter's pipeline: SPSA gives the same accuracy at 27–41% of
  the cost. For improved it's the clear choice (same AUPRC, lower variance, 3.7×
  cheaper). For reverse2 it's a small accuracy-for-cost trade.

  Code status:
  - The cost fix is in peter.py: it saves the final SPSA execution count at the
    end of training, so future runs report it correctly without manual
    correction.
  - Results are in vqc_runs/cmp_adam/ and vqc_runs/cmp_spsa/ (test_results.csv,
    cost.csv, checkpoints and MLP encoders).

  2. ROC and precision-recall curves

  The figure has four panels: ROC on top, precision-recall below, at p = 0.01
  and 0.05. Each curve is the mean over 5 seeds, with a band from worst to best
  seed. "Logistic regression" here is the reference on the baseline's 4 PCA
  angles (0.703). The other pipelines have their own references: quantile 0.730,
  MLP angles 0.720, linear angles 0.701.

  ┌──────────────────────┬───────────┬───────────┬───────────┬───────────┐
  │        model         │ AUROC, p  │ AUROC, p  │ AUPRC, p  │ AUPRC, p  │
  │                      │  = 0.01   │  = 0.05   │  = 0.01   │  = 0.05   │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
  │ baseline (Task 1     │ 0.968     │ 0.955 ±   │ 0.564     │ 0.405 ±   │
  │ VQC)                 │           │ 0.024     │           │ 0.177     │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
  │ linear-encoder VQC   │ 0.970     │ 0.967     │ 0.680     │ 0.662 ±   │
  │ (reverse2)           │           │           │           │ 0.058     │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
  │ MLP-encoder VQC      │ 0.971     │ 0.971     │ 0.727     │ 0.725 ±   │

  ├──────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
  │ kan-quant VQC        │ 0.973     │ 0.973     │ 0.732     │ 0.737 ±   │
  │                      │           │           │           │ 0.028     │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
  │ logistic regression  │ 0.976     │ 0.976     │ 0.703     │ 0.703     │
  └──────────────────────┴───────────┴───────────┴───────────┴───────────┘

  What the curves show
  - AUROC barely separates the models (0.955–0.976), and the logistic regression
    even has the highest. With 0.17% fraud, almost all of the ROC area comes
    from regions no fraud team would operate in. That's why AUPRC is the primary
    metric, and the figure makes the point visually. On the log-scale axis, the
    differences are all between false-positive rates of 10⁻⁴ and 10⁻³. (The
    faint diagonal is the random classifier; it looks curved only because of the
    log scale.)
  - In that low false-alarm region, kan-quant is ahead. It reaches about 60%
    recall at a false-positive rate of about 1.5 × 10⁻⁴, before any other model.
    That's about 8 false alarms in the 57k test transactions.
  - Precision-recall: kan-quant has the highest plateau, about 0.85–0.87
    precision from 15% to 75% recall. The logistic regression wins only at the
    very top of the ranking (recall below about 17%, precision 1.0). The MLP
    model is strongest around the cliff at 80–87% recall.
  - The baseline collapses at p = 0.05. Its band spans most of the plot, showing
    the instability across seeds. Every other model's curves are almost
    identical between p = 0.01 and p = 0.05.
  - The linear encoder sits between the baseline and the MLP/KAN models, and its
    band (orange) is wider. That matches the table above.

  The ROC legends overlap the curves slightly at the top left. If you need the
  figure for a slide, I can move the legends below the panels.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● I removed the top title. The updated figure is saved over
  task2_final_roc_pr.png. Each panel still says which set and noise level it
  shows ("ROC on test, p = 0.05"), and the curves are unchanged.

  Does the PDF say which part of the data to compute the curves on? Not for the
  curves specifically. The brief never mentions ROC or precision-recall curves
  at all. But it's unambiguous about where results come from, and the curves are
  just pictures of those results:
  - Dataset section: "Results must be reported on the test set of the common
    split, which keeps the original class distribution."
  - Evaluation protocol, Data: "Balancing, encoding, architecture, epochs and
    threshold are fitted or chosen on training and validation data only; the 
    test set is used only for final results."
  - Evaluation protocol, Metrics: "test AUPRC as the primary metric … AUROC;
    Spearman ρ between noisy and noiseless test scores; precision, recall, F1
    and MCC at the threshold."

  AUPRC is the area under the precision-recall curve, and AUROC is the area
  under the ROC curve. The protocol asks for both on the test set, so drawing
  the curves on test is consistent with it. The figure's legend values match the
  official results tables exactly.

  The one thing to keep straight: those test curves are for reporting only.
  Anything you choose (threshold, model, epoch, hyperparameters) must come from
  validation, which is what the pipeline does. If you ever wanted curves to help
  choose something, for example where to set an operating point, those should
  be drawn on the validation set instead.

  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Logistic          │ 1 (no circuit)    │ 1       │ 1 · 1 · 1 · 1 · 1   │
  │ regression        │                   │         │                     │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Linear-encoder    │ 0.875 ± 0.090     │ 0.761   │ 0.96 · 0.76 · 0.96  │
  │ VQC               │                   │         │ · 0.87 · 0.82       │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ MLP-encoder VQC   │ 0.905 ± 0.076     │ 0.774   │ 0.95 · 0.92 · 0.97  │
  │                   │                   │         │ · 0.77 · 0.91       │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ KAN + quantile    │ 0.978 ± 0.031     │ 0.923   │ 0.99 · 0.99 · 1.00  │
  │ VQC               │                   │         │ · 0.92 · 0.99       │
  └───────────────────┴───────────────────┴─────────┴─────────────────────┘

  How to read it
  - The KAN + quantile model is the most robust by a clear margin. Its average
    ranking is almost untouched at maximum noise. Even its worst seed (0.923)
    beats the average of the linear and MLP models. Unlike the AUPRC differences
    between KAN and MLP, this gap is substantial.
  - The baseline's ranking really degrades. On its worst seed (0.481) the noisy
    ordering of transactions has little in common with the noiseless one. That's
    the effect the brief describes for the 93-gate circuit.
  - The logistic regression is trivially 1: it has no circuit, so noise can't
    affect it. I labelled it "1 (no circuit)" so it doesn't read as a competing
    result.
  - The linear and MLP models use the reverse2 circuit, which has fewer noise 
    locations than the KAN circuit (21 vs 25), yet their rankings are less
    stable. So the difference comes from the encoders and training, not from
    circuit size. Their MinMax/MLP angles leave many transactions with nearly
    equal scores, and noise reshuffles those; the quantile spread separates them
    better.

  On the slide, the best quantum model is bold and highlighted in the mean and
  worst-seed columns. The footnote explains what low ρ values mean, and the
  speaker notes give the one-line takeaway. The previous slide (AUPRC) is
  unchanged.

  Circuit (P · gates · noise loc.): 15 · 20 · 25
  Training: 565,440
  Validation: 1,708,860
  Total (shots): 2,274,300 (2.33 B)
  vs baseline: 80%

  P is the number of trainable circuit parameters. The classical encoders
  (linear, MLP, KAN) and the logistic regression run classically, so they cost
  no circuit executions.

  Read it carefully: the two pipelines validate very differently, and that 
  dominates the totals.
  - Your pipeline (baseline, KAN): the full 56,962-row validation set after
    every epoch. That's 1.71M executions, 60–75% of each total.
  - Peter's pipeline (linear, MLP): a 5,099-row validation subset every 3
    epochs, only 20k executions. He also trains 12 epochs instead of 30.
  - So the 9.4% vs 80% gap is mostly about training and validation settings, not
    the circuits. For the circuits themselves, compare the training column:
    1.15M (baseline), 565k (KAN, 30 epochs), 248k (linear/MLP, 12 epochs).
  - The KAN model with cheap validation costs 653,700 (23% of the baseline),
    noted in the slide's footnote. At 12 epochs it would be about 226k for
    training, comparable to Peter's.

  Other notes
  - The KAN row counts 608 examples per epoch: 19 stratified batches of 32 from
    the full training set. That's why its training cost is 31 × 608 × 30 and not
    × 590.
  - These are formula counts. With qml.Tracker, the baseline's training drops to
    725,700 (total 2,434,560), because PennyLane skips the 12 parameters that
    can't reach the readout. For the KAN circuit both counts agree. Peter's
    pipeline only reports the formula, so I used the formula everywhere for a
    consistent comparison.

  The three slides now cover AUPRC at every noise level, ranking robustness (ρ)
  at p = 0.05, and cost. The speaker notes on this slide explain that validation
  dominates, so you can make that point when presenting.

● I wrote kan_vqc.py in the project folder, and the tests are running.

  What's in kan_vqc.py (about 400 lines, standalone, Romanian comments like your
  other files):
  - Data: the official split (split_indices.npz). Undersampling only to fit the
    encoder (PCA + quantile transform on the balanced rows) and the
    logistic-regression reference.
  - Model: the 4-qubit angle circuit with the per-feature KAN, input/output
    scaling, light-cone pruning and gate merging (20 gates, 25 noise locations).
    All parameters live in one tensor, so Adam and SPSA treat them identically.
  - Training: on the full training set, with stratified, weighted batches.
    - --optimizer adam: exact gradient, 30 epochs × 19 batches by default.
    - --optimizer spsa: Spall's gains, calibrated first step, q = 2 estimates
      averaged per step, early stopping.
  - Validation:
    - --val full: the whole validation set after every epoch.
    - --val cheap: all frauds plus a fraction of legitimate transactions, with
      weighted AUPRC. Set the fraction with --val-neg-rate and the frequency
      with --val-every.
  - Evaluation: the official protocol at p = 0, 0.001, 0.01 and 0.05, with the
    threshold chosen on noisy validation at each p, test metrics, Spearman ρ,
    and the logistic regression on the same angles.
  - Cost ledger: training and validation executions and shots, by the formula
    and by qml.Tracker.
  - Outputs: <out>_runs.csv, <out>_summary.csv and <out>_cost.csv. --quick is a
    fast smoke test.

  The quick test passed for Adam with full validation, Adam with cheap
  validation, and SPSA.

  Running now (4 parallel processes):

  run: kan_repro
  settings: defaults, seed 0
  purpose: must match the earlier angle_kan_full_quant seed-0 result exactly, to

  verify the extraction changed nothing
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  run: kan_cheap
  settings: cheap validation every epoch, 30 epochs
  purpose: your model at about 27% of the baseline's cost (expected 0.717)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  run: kan_petermatch
  settings: 12 epochs, validation every 3 epochs on all frauds + 5,000
  legitimate
  purpose: the same training and validation budget as Peter's runs, so cost and
  accuracy compare directly
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  run: kan_spsa
  settings: SPSA with cheap validation
  purpose: the lowest-cost option

  I'd expect about 30–45 minutes, mostly the noisy evaluations. When they
  finish, I'll check the reproduction first. Then I'll put all four next to
  Peter's runs (MLP and linear encoder, Adam and SPSA, improved and reverse2) in
  one table: AUPRC at each noise level, ρ at p = 0.05, and training +
  validation executions and shots.

  The MLP models use Peter's reverse2 circuit. The angle SPSA model is
  angle_pruned_spsa; it also has a one-off pilot of 172k executions.

  How to read it
  - Best accuracy: KAN + quantile with Adam, best at every noise level. But its
    cost is high (80%), because it scores the full validation set every epoch.
  - Best accuracy for the cost: MLP with SPSA. 0.713 at p = 0.05 for 3.8% of the
    baseline, with very stable rankings (ρ 0.987).
  - Lowest cost: angle VQC with SPSA. 3.2% of the baseline, for 0.662.
  - KAN does not work with SPSA. Two of five seeds fail: 0.009 (an inverted
    ranking SPSA never escaped) and 0.434. The other three reach 0.62–0.72. Its
    ρ of 0.994 is the highest in the table, but that only means noise doesn't
    change its ranking; for two seeds that ranking is poor. The slide's footnote
    says this explicitly. As before, SPSA struggles with the KAN's 34 extra
    classical parameters. It would need its own step-size tuning, or Adam for
    the classical part and SPSA only for the circuit.

  The like-for-like cost comparison with Peter's pipeline (from the other test
  runs; not in the table, since you asked for the normal and SPSA versions):

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────┬───────────┐
  │                       │                         │ AUPRC at │           │
  │          run          │        settings         │   p =    │   cost    │
  │                       │                         │   0.05   │           │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ KAN, Peter's budget   │ 12 epochs, validation   │          │ 246,572   │
  │ (kan_petermatch)      │ every 3 epochs on 5,000 │ 0.699    │ (8.6%)    │
  │                       │  legitimate             │          │           │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ MLP Adam (Peter)      │ same budget             │ 0.725    │ 268,196   │
  │                       │                         │          │ (9.4%)    │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ KAN, cheap validation │ 30 epochs, validation   │          │ 653,700   │
  │  (kan_cheap)          │ every epoch on 5% of    │ 0.717    │ (23%)     │
  │                       │ legitimate              │          │           │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────┴───────────┘

  - With identical budgets, the KAN model costs about 8% less but scores about 
    0.026 lower than Peter's MLP. It needs its 30 epochs: its best checkpoints
    come late (epochs 6–20), and cutting to 12 epochs loses accuracy.
  - So at a budget of about 250k executions, Peter's MLP encoder is the better 
    choice; the KAN model's advantage appears only with more training.

  Verification: kan_vqc.py reproduces the original main.py results exactly: a
  difference of 0.0 on every metric and noise level for seed 0, and on every
  seed for cheap validation. The extracted file is a faithful standalone version
  of your model. Its outputs are in the project folder: kan_repro_*,
  kan_cheap_*, kan_petermatch_* and kan_spsa_* (runs, summary and cost CSVs).

    - that KAN with SPSA fails on 2 of 5 seeds;
    - why SPSA saves less in Peter's pipeline (it already trains cheaply, and
      SPSA validates more often);
    - the angle model's one-off 172k pilot.
  - The speaker notes give the takeaway: SPSA cuts training executions 4–9×; it
    costs at most about 0.01 AUPRC on the angle and MLP models, but is unstable
    with the KAN's extra classical parameters.

  One thing to point out when presenting: the improved MLP with SPSA has the
  best AUPRC change (+0.003) but a weak ρ (0.767 ± 0.356). One seed's ranking
  collapses under noise (ρ 0.136) while its AUPRC stays fine. The ρ column shows
  that next to the AUPRC, so the trade-off is visible.

  2. ρ at maximum noise
  3. cost ledger

● The table is ready as task2_table_kan_scaling.pptx, a one-slide deck with a
  native, editable table. It passed validation and the rendering looks clean.
  It's also in task2_table_kan_scaling.csv.

  Angle scaling on the KAN VQC: MinMax vs quantile (same circuit, training and
  seeds; only the angle mapping differs; test set, mean ± std over 5 seeds)

  Angle scaling: MinMax
  Legit cluster width (rad, middle 80%): 0.03 – 0.09
  AUPRC p = 0: 0.696 ± 0.032
  AUPRC p = 0.01: 0.697 ± 0.036
  AUPRC p = 0.05: 0.698 ± 0.044
  ρ at p = 0.05: 0.992 ± 0.013
  F1 at p = 0.05: 0.727 ± 0.026
  Logistic regression (same inputs): 0.703 ± 0.010
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Angle scaling: Quantile
  Legit cluster width (rad, middle 80%): 1.38 – 1.83
  AUPRC p = 0: 0.731 ± 0.029
  AUPRC p = 0.01: 0.732 ± 0.029
  AUPRC p = 0.05: 0.737 ± 0.028
  ρ at p = 0.05: 0.978 ± 0.031
  F1 at p = 0.05: 0.765 ± 0.019
  Logistic regression (same inputs): 0.730 ± 0.011
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Angle scaling: Δ quantile − MinMax
  Legit cluster width (rad, middle 80%): ≈ 20× wider
  AUPRC p = 0: +0.035
  AUPRC p = 0.01: +0.035 [−0.001, +0.070]
  AUPRC p = 0.05: +0.039 [+0.006, +0.074]
  ρ at p = 0.05: −0.014
  F1 at p = 0.05: +0.038
  Logistic regression (same inputs): +0.027

  The intervals in the Δ row come from 400 paired, stratified bootstrap
  resamples of the test set.

  What it shows
  - Quantile scaling helps the KAN model, by about +0.04 AUPRC at both headline
    noise levels.
    - At p = 0.05 the gain is statistically clear: the interval excludes zero.
    - At p = 0.01 it's borderline: the lower end is −0.001, positive in 97% of
      resamples.
  - F1 at the validation threshold improves just as much (+0.038). That's
    useful, since F1 was the KAN model's weak spot with MinMax.
  - The mechanism is in the second column. With MinMax, the dense cluster of
    legitimate transactions gets only 0.03–0.09 rad, because fraud outliers
    stretch the range. The quantile transform spreads it over 1.4–1.8 rad, so
    the circuit can actually tell those transactions apart.
  - Robustness costs a little: ρ drops from 0.992 to 0.978, still very high.
  - The classical reference improves too (+0.027). So it's an input fix, not a
    quantum-only gain. The slide's footnote says this, and the gap to the
    logistic regression stays small either way (−0.005 with MinMax, +0.007 with
    quantile).

  The best value in each column is highlighted, including ρ for MinMax, so the
  trade-off is visible. The speaker notes give the one-sentence takeaway.

  - task2_table_spsa.pptx: Adam vs SPSA, original version;
  - task2_table_spsa_split.pptx: Adam vs SPSA with the cost split into training
    and validation;
  - task2_table_kan_scaling.pptx: this one.

  How to read it
  - Noise locations are the depolarizing channels the noise model inserts: one
    per single-qubit gate, two per CNOT. So 126 = 60 single-qubit gates + 2 × 33
    CNOTs for the baseline.
  - Every Task 2 model cuts the baseline's noise exposure by about 80% (126 →
    21–25 noise locations, 33 → 5–6 CNOTs, depth 45 → 8–9). That's why they
    barely lose accuracy at p = 0.05 while the baseline collapses. The speaker
    notes make this point.
  - reverse2 has the fewest gates because it fuses three rotations into one
    native Rot gate. Each Rot still counts as one noise location, so its 21 is a
    fair count.
  - The KAN circuit has the fewest CNOTs (5) but more single-qubit gates, so it
    ends up with 25 noise locations, slightly above reverse2. Its 34 classical
    parameters (input/output scaling and the KAN curves) aren't in the circuit,
    so they add no noise. The footnote says this, along with how noise locations
    are counted.
  - Linear and MLP share the same circuit; only their classical encoder differs.

  The best value per column among the quantum models is highlighted in bold. The
  deck now has six slides:
  1. AUPRC at every noise level, with total executions
  2. ρ at maximum noise
  3. cost ledger
  4. accuracy/robustness/cost summary
  5. Adam vs SPSA
  6. circuit size and noise locations

❯ What can you tell me about the balancing done on the 3 hybrid models          

● I'll check the actual code of both pipelines before answering, rather than go
  from memory:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● All three hybrid models optimise the same thing: a class-balanced objective,
  in which frauds and legitimate transactions weigh 50/50. They balance at
  different stages, though, and the linear/MLP pipeline and the KAN pipeline do
  it in opposite places.

  The data: the training split has 170,883 transactions, of which only 295 are
  fraud (0.17%). Validation and test keep that real ratio in every pipeline;
  balancing only ever touches training data.

  Where each model balances

  Stage: Classical encoder
  Linear encoder (Peter): trained on all 170,883 training rows, with class
  weighting: pos_weight = 170,588 / 295 ≈ 578 in the loss
  MLP encoder (Peter): the same: all rows, pos_weight ≈ 578
  KAN + quantile (yours): PCA + quantile transform fitted on the 590-row 
  undersampled set (295 fraud + 295 legit)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stage: Quantum circuit (Adam)
  Linear encoder (Peter): trained on the 590-row undersampled set, unweighted
  loss
  MLP encoder (Peter): the same: 590 rows
  KAN + quantile (yours): trained on all training rows: each batch is 16 frauds
  +
  16 legitimate, weighted 0.5/16 each, so the loss is the balanced 50/50
  objective without discarding data
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stage: Quantum circuit (SPSA)
  Linear encoder (Peter): –
  MLP encoder (Peter): all rows, the same stratified batches (my addition,
  --spsa-data full)
  KAN + quantile (yours): all rows, stratified batches
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stage: Logistic regression reference
  Linear encoder (Peter): 590 undersampled rows, on the encoder's angles
  MLP encoder (Peter): the same
  KAN + quantile (yours): 590 undersampled rows, on the quantile angles
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stage: Checkpoint choice
  Linear encoder (Peter): all 99 validation frauds + 5,000 legitimate, weighted
  AUPRC
  MLP encoder (Peter): the same
  KAN + quantile (yours): full validation set (or the weighted subset with cheap

  validation)

  What follows from this

  1. The two pipelines are mirror images.
     - Peter's classical part learns from all 170k transactions, while his
       quantum part sees only 590.
     - Yours is the reverse: the encoder is fitted on 590 rows, and the circuit
       sees thousands of different legitimate transactions over training (9,120
       draws out of 170,588).
     - It partly explains why his angles carry more signal: the logistic
       regression on his MLP angles reaches 0.720, against 0.703 on PCA angles.
       In your model the gain comes from the circuit's training data instead.
     - It also means his MLP encoder does a lot of the work. That's allowed
       ("classical layers may prepare the inputs"), but worth saying.
  2. The frauds are the real bottleneck everywhere. There are only 295 training
     frauds. In your model each fraud is reused about 31 times per run (9,120
     fraud draws). Peter's encoder sees each one roughly once per epoch, but
     weighted 578×, which makes its gradients noisy; early stopping on
     validation keeps that in check. No balancing method can create more fraud
     examples. That's ultimately why everything plateaus around 0.70–0.74 AUPRC.
  3. The balanced objective distorts probabilities but not rankings. Training as
     if fraud were 50% makes the scores far too high as probabilities (Dal
     Pozzolo et al., the brief's reference [2]). That doesn't affect AUPRC or
     Spearman ρ, which only use the order. The F1 threshold is re-chosen on
     validation, at the real fraud rate. So it's harmless here, as long as
     nobody reads the scores as real fraud probabilities. Your loss-weight test
     confirms the choice: weighting the loss closer to the real rate (fraud
     share 0.25) was significantly worse (−0.015).
  4. The Adam vs SPSA comparison for Peter's models has a confounder. His Adam
     runs train the circuit on the 590-row balanced set, but the SPSA version I
     added trains on all rows with stratified batches (the default --spsa-data 
     full). So those rows on the SPSA slide differ in data as well as optimiser.
     To isolate the optimiser, rerun his SPSA with --spsa-data balanced (about
     15 minutes for both circuits), or say so in a footnote. Your own Adam vs
     SPSA pairs don't have this problem: both use the full data.
  5. Seed variance comes partly from balancing. In all three models the
     undersampled set changes with the seed. In your model it changes the PCA
     and quantile fit, and so the angles themselves. In Peter's it changes the
     circuit's training rows. Part of each model's ± over seeds is "which 295
     legitimate transactions were drawn", not just the circuit's random start.
  6. Rule compliance: all fine. Every balancing step and encoder fit uses
     training data only. Validation is used only to choose checkpoints,
     early-stop, and pick thresholds. The test set stays at the real
     distribution. The brief also asks for balancing to be "fully documented",
     so I'd put this table (or a condensed version) in the presentation.

  Shall I rerun Peter's SPSA with balanced data, to clean up the comparison in
  point 4?
